# Command and Conquer: Generals: Zero Hour: Reforged

EA opened the source of Generals and Zero Hour, the game that is still on sale and still runs on
Steam. This build is that source with the bugs the game shipped with fixed and everything below added.

**126 changes. ~580 engine source files ported. 14 automated test suites. Around 60 original bugs
found and fixed â€” EA's own, not port damage.**

---

## Zero Hour on a Mac, on Linux and on the Steam Deck

- The game runs on Apple silicon Macs and on Linux, the Steam Deck included, as a program built for that machine. There is no Wine or Proton underneath. It draws through Metal on a Mac and through Vulkan on Linux, from the same Zero Hour files you already own.
- The first start finds your game. It looks in your Steam libraries, `~/Games`, `/Applications`, and CrossOver and Whisky bottles, and asks for the folder only when it comes up empty.
- One command builds it. `./build-macos.sh` leaves a Mac app ready to open and `./build-linux.sh` a folder ready to start, and each checks your machine first and names the package to install for anything missing. On Linux it can make an AppImage, a .deb, an .rpm, an Arch package and a Flatpak, none of them carrying EA's files, and the Flatpak has played a full check match on a Steam Deck.
- One game on every machine. A replay recorded on a Mac plays back on Linux and on Windows to the same checksum, and the other way round, and a Mac and a Linux PC have played a LAN match together without once disagreeing. A Mac or Linux game could not join a Windows one: the check every machine runs before a network game failed, because the Mac and Linux copies of the game's data files ended each line differently from Windows'. They agree now. A match between Windows and a Mac has not been played yet.
- Text on the Linux menus is as wide as on Windows. Linux has no Arial, and the font standing in for it reports a wider average letter, so every menu font the game sets to a fixed width came out a quarter narrower than it should, the "Generals" lettering and the menu buttons among them. Each is measured against Arial's own figure now, and lands within one percent of Windows.
- The game no longer crashes on some Linux PCs as its window opens. The game hands out memory its own way, and on Linux the graphics driver gets its memory from the game too. Some drivers, AMD's on Debian 12 among them, need every piece lined up on a 16-byte boundary, and the game's lined up on 4, so the start died or went through depending on where the pieces happened to fall. On Linux everything outside the game's own pools now comes from the system, which lines it up as the driver expects.
- `./build-linux.sh` stopped on Linux Mint and Arch after the centred Classic interface went in: the new code compared layout names with a Windows-only spelling that GCC on Linux has never heard of. It uses the name every platform knows now, and so do the two debug-build spots with the same habit. Nobody has rebuilt on Linux since, so whether anything else stands in the way is not known yet.
- Windows on Arm laptops, the Snapdragon ones, get a build made for them. Microsoft never shipped those machines the library the game loads its textures and shaders through, so the game brings its own, the texture code the Mac and Linux versions already run. Switched on in place of Microsoft's library on an ordinary Windows PC, it draws the same battlefields, with no tree, house or patch of ground going missing. Nobody has played it on a Snapdragon laptop yet.
- Thanks to ilyasakin, whose port this is.

## The command bar moves to the middle

- The command bar is one console standing in the middle of the bottom edge: a taller radar, your selection and the build grid side by side, with the power bar over the grid. The idle worker and rank keys sit in a row over your selection, and in multiplayer the smoke signals join them. The red under-attack lamp is gone: the ping on the radar, the voice and the line in the event feed already tell you where you are hit, and the lamp only blinked about it a second time.
- Your general's powers grow out of the console's right end, a column for each F key with its powers stacked up it, and only the powers you have take any room.
- A single unit's name and health stand beside its portrait, and a veteran gets his gold stars there too. Under them sit its main weapon's damage, damage a second and range, and how much of the damage from gunfire, tank shells and rockets its armour stops: +50% in green for a unit that takes half, a minus in red for one that takes more. A Terrorist, or a Combat Bike with one riding it, used to read 999,999 damage and millions a second, the figures of the stand-in charge that kills the rider so his real bomb can go off. They read the bomb now, 500 for the Terrorist's dynamite and 700 for the bike's, and a bomb that goes off once has no damage a second. Your own units and your allies' show as they stand, so a veteran's bonus, a Composite Armor upgrade or Hold the Line shows the moment it lands. An enemy's show only what his veterancy stars already tell you, never his upgrades or battle plans. Click an enemy and his health reads as a share, 64%, because 512/800 would tell you he bought Composite Armor; the upgrade icons under his portrait stay grey for everything his side researched, where retail lit Composite Armor or Supply Lines for anyone who clicked, and light only for what the unit visibly carries, a drone or an Overlord's turret. A group shows every type it holds in the same box, five to a row. Click a type there and only those stay selected; shift-click it and they leave the selection, so the two Rangers that tagged along with your tanks are one click from gone. Watching a match with nothing selected, the empty portrait box is gone and the console is the radar alone; click a unit and the box comes back. Following nobody, the experience bar beside the radar is gone too, since there is no general's rank to fill it.
- The match clock hangs from the middle of the top edge with your money in its own box under it. Superweapon countdowns in the top right corner are as big as your general's power buttons. Hold Tab and the scoreboard drops down from it.
- Peace time has its own plate under your money, so the truce clock never pushes the match clock aside. When the last ten seconds start, the countdown hangs straight off that plate.
- A superweapon built, charged or fired anywhere on the map flashes its name in big letters under the clock: "Nuclear Missile launched". Yours are white, an ally's blue, an enemy's red. Each line stays four seconds and fades out. When several land at once they queue and hurry through, two seconds each with one more waiting and one second with a pile behind it, so the last news never sits stale.
- Your production queue stands on the console over your selection, five to a row, each row only as long as what is in it. The event feed runs down from the menu key in the top left corner, and the chat sits under it.
- Point at the attack, hold position or move key and a card says what it does, in English or Turkish. Those three are painted onto the grid rather than given to a unit, so the game had no card for them and showed nothing. The card stands on top of the command bar like every other button's.
- HUD Size grows the console from its middle, and on a narrow screen it stops at the size that still fits.
- The grid under a building you are placing is fainter and its lines thinner, and the red squares where nothing can stand are lighter, so the ground under them shows through. Untick grid placement on the Controls page and the lines go altogether.
- Hold Ctrl while placing a building and grid placement flips for as long as the key is down. With the box unticked the building snaps to the grid and the lines come up, so one flush row is a held key away; with it ticked you get a free hand for the odd building that has to sit between two squares. It works the same in Classic.
- Your general's promotion screen opens in the middle of the screen, in the Classic interface as well, instead of hanging from the top edge. The battlefield dims behind it, and a click anywhere off the screen closes it without selecting or ordering anything underneath.

## Groups attack-move at their own speed

- An attack move used to hold the whole group to its slowest member when you held Ctrl on the click, and the computer's groups did that every time. A damaged truck walked the tanks in. Each unit now goes at its own speed. A replay recorded before this, of a group attack-moving that way, will not play back the same.

## Veterancy goes to everyone who fought

- A kill used to promote only the unit that fired the last shot. Two Crusaders could grind an Overlord down to a sliver and a passing Humvee's rocket took every point of it. Now the killing blow earns a quarter and the rest is shared out among everything that hurt the target in the last ten seconds, by how much of its health each one took off. A unit that died before the kill leaves its share to the killer. Healing counts too: an Emperor, or an Overlord or Helix with a Propaganda Tower, earns for every friendly unit it patches up, half of that unit's worth as a kill for a full repair and less for less. The kill on the scoreboard and your general's promotion points still go to the last shot. Replays recorded before this change play out differently.

## Tanks and cars drive like vehicles

- A Crusader used to reach top speed in a thirtieth of a second, on its very first frame moving twice as fast as it ever could again, and stopped dead the frame it was told to. Every tank, car and bike now picks up speed over about a second and brakes over most of one: told to stop at full speed, a Crusader rolls 12 more units and a Technical 26, where both used to halt on the spot. A vehicle sees the end of its move coming and is already braking for it.
- Vehicles slow down for a corner they can see coming. EA cut a tank to 60% of its speed on every frame near a turn in its route and threw it back to full speed in between, so a column on a winding road lurched along in a sawtooth. Now a vehicle brakes before a bend by as much as the bend needs, turns through it on the move and speeds up out of it. A tank still stops and swings round on the spot for anything sharper than a right angle, at half the rate it used to; an Overlord sent straight behind itself comes all the way round in about 2.4 seconds.
- Sent a short way behind itself, a tank backs up. It used to spin round on the spot and drive off nose first every time. Anything sent within three lengths behind it now reverses at a bit under half speed, and so does a car that has a reverse gear. A Humvee sent a long way behind does a three-point turn instead of pirouetting in its own length. Cars used to reverse at full speed; they now back up at 45% of it.
- A car turns no tighter than a car can. EA turned a Quad Cannon round in less than its own length about its rear axle, so its middle swung sideways at twice the speed it drove. A car's turn is held to a circle one and a half lengths across at speed, and only when that circle cannot reach the spot it was sent to does it crawl round a tight one instead, so it still gets everywhere it used to. Without a reverse gear that costs time: a Quad Cannon sent 50 behind itself takes 4.8 seconds where it took 2.2.
- A vehicle that had just parked used to start its next move with a lurch, and arrived crabbing sideways into its spot. Both are gone.
- Players told us the tanks felt sluggish off the line, and they were right. Every vehicle you can build, on all three sides and in all nine generals' armies, now pulls away and brakes a quarter harder than it did. A Crusader is at full speed 1.17 seconds after the order instead of 1.37, an Overlord after 1.27 instead of 1.5, and a Humvee sent across a short stretch of ground arrives an eighth of a second sooner. Top speeds are unchanged, and soldiers, aircraft and boats are untouched. Replays recorded before this change will not play back.
- They turn faster as well. Every vehicle you can build swings round a quarter faster than it did, and none turns slower than 120 degrees a second, so the Overlord gains most: sent somewhere straight behind it, an Overlord is rolling at speed 2.5 seconds after the order where it took 4.4, and a Crusader after 1.77 seconds instead of 1.93. The Emperor comes round twice as fast as before, the SCUD Launcher more than that. Turrets turn exactly as they did. Replays recorded before this change will not play back, and this version cannot join a game hosted on an older one.
- Groups get where they are going as fast as before. Twenty Crusaders crossing a generated map arrived on average within 3% of the old time over eight maps, and the last of them a fifth of a second later. A replay recorded before this change plays back differently.

## Soldiers turn and stop on their feet

- Send a running Ranger back the way he came and he used to slide backwards past his own nose for a sixth of a second while he swung round. Now his feet carry him round with the turn, and past a right angle he plants them and pivots. On a gentle bend he barely slows: through 30 degrees a Ranger keeps 85% of his pace where he used to drop to 68%, and a 90 degree corner costs him half the time it did.
- A soldier told to stop takes three short steps to a halt instead of freezing mid-stride, about one more step of ground. Arriving at the end of a move looks the same way. He still starts walking as quickly after the click as he always did.
- Infantry on attack move look before they step. A Ranger used to walk three paces toward the move point, stop and turn on an enemy that had been in plain sight, and a Red Guard came at it from the side and pivoted on the spot before he fired. Both now walk straight at the enemy and arrive facing it. A replay recorded before this change plays back differently.

## Tanks shoot on the move

- A turret that picked its own target on the move fired once and then let go of it until its next look round, two seconds later for most units. A Technical driving past four Troop Crawlers hit them twice. It now hits every 6 frames, 11 times, as fast as it fires standing still. On the same drive a Quad Cannon went from 28 hits to 53 and a Gattling Tank from 28 to 50, and an Overlord's second shell comes 10 frames after its first instead of 38.
- Told to move in the middle of a fight, a tank or car with a turret keeps shooting what it was attacking for as long as the target is alive and in range, the way helicopters already did. A Technical pulled back from four Troop Crawlers used to fall silent for two and a half seconds and no longer stops at all. A Marauder used to wait almost four seconds past its reload before the next shell and now waits half a second. A move order still never starts a new fight.
- The Marauder's turret turns. The model always had one, but the tank swung its whole body round to aim and could not shoot on the move. The turret turns 100 degrees a second, as fast as the Scorpion's: driving past four Troop Crawlers a Marauder used to fire nothing and now lands three shells, and circling them it kills all four where it never fired. The turret is slower round than the hull was, so a target appearing straight behind a parked Marauder takes its first shell most of a second later than before.
- The Gattling Tank opens fire 15 degrees before its guns are on the target and walks the stream onto it, where it used to wait until it was within 2 degrees. The Gattling Cannon defence and the Strategy Center's bombardment cannon do the same. Circling four Troop Crawlers, a Gattling Tank hit them 208 times where it hit 102, and 12 of the extra hits come from the earlier start.
- The Gattling cannon on an Overlord's back turns at the Gattling Tank's 180 degrees a second instead of 60 and opens fire the same way. A target appearing behind the Overlord is under fire 22 frames sooner and took 66 hits where it took 41.
- A replay recorded before this change plays back differently.
- Tell a tank to fire at the ground and it fires from where it stands. On any slope the game read the spot as hidden behind the very hill it was lying on, so a tank with the spot well inside its range drove off looking for a better angle, swinging its hull round under its own turret, and fired on the way. Pulled off a building to shell the ground 140 behind them, a Battlemaster took 84 frames to its first shell on the new spot and now takes 45, which is how long its turret needs to come round, and a pair of Scorpions that used to wander about 200 away over eight seconds now stay where they were put. Replays recorded before this play back differently.

## Point defense lasers back on the planes, in clips on the tanks

- The Air Force General's King Raptor, Aurora, Stealth Fighter, B-3 and Chinooks have their point defense lasers again, and so does the Boss General's Raptor. From 2.4.0 they dropped flares instead; the flares are gone, and the lasers fire the way they did in EA's game, with no clip and no upgrade to buy. Two King Raptors making seven passes over a Stinger Site burned down 52 and 54 Stingers and came home with 360 and 390 health of 480. A Chinook sitting on its pad shoots down missiles again too, and the laser takes on a SCUD or a Tomahawk near these aircraft, which no flare ever fooled. Countermeasures works as it did in EA's game.
- A missile a point defense laser hits now goes up in a proper burst: a bright flash, sparks falling away and a short orange blast on top of EA's red flash and scrap of casing. It is gone in a second and leaves no smoke, so a run of intercepts does not stack a grey column over the tank. This goes for every point defense laser: the Paladin, the Avenger, the Air Force General's planes and Chinooks, and the Superweapon General's drone.
- The Paladin and the Avenger keep their point defense lasers, and you can see them again. In 2.4.0 they fired an invisible charge: a puff at the hull and a missile bursting in the air with nothing between them. The beam is back, drawn from the same spot on the tank EA drew it from, and a missile it touches still goes in one shot. What 2.4.0 changed stays. The lasers fire in short bursts: a Paladin gets two shots and four seconds to recharge, each of the Avenger's two lasers gets four and three seconds. Under five Tank Hunters an Avenger stops 8 missiles where EA's endless laser stopped 14 or 16, but it dies on the same frame, because fifteen hits kill it either way. A Paladin under four Rocket Buggies stops 4 rockets of 36, as it always did.
- The Paladin's laser also burns any soldier who comes within 65 of the tank, and a soldier it kills burns up again instead of being thrown by a blast. It has a second laser of its own for that, with its own two shots, so firing at a soldier never leaves the tank empty when a missile arrives.
- Lasers glow now. The Paladin's and the Avenger's point defense, the Laser General's Crusader, Paladin, Avenger and turrets, and the point defense drone each draw a white-hot core down the middle of the beam and a soft halo in the beam's own colour along its whole length, so a pink Laser Avenger beam sits in a pink haze from the tank to its target. On the default renderer the core spills into the screen's bloom; on the Direct3D 9 one the halo carries the glow by itself. The Avenger's marking laser, the one that paints a target for the rest of your army, is not a weapon and keeps EA's thin blue line without a halo. The Glow setting decides how wide the halo burns, and Off gives you EA's plain beam back. The halo now rounds off past both ends of the beam instead of stopping in a straight cut across it. EA's own beams, the marking laser included, stop blinking: their texture scrolled half its height every frame, so at a steady 30 frames a second they were drawn on one frame and dark the next, in the 2003 game too. Now they hold the lit middle of the texture and show on every frame. Only the picture changed: damage, range and timing are what they were, and replays play back the same.
- A replay recorded before this change plays back differently.

## Helicopters fly like helicopters

- Every helicopter in the game, on every side and for every general, used to drive like a tank with rotors. Told to go somewhere behind it, a Comanche swung its nose all the way round on the spot and only then flew off nose first. Now it sets off at once wherever it is sent and brings its nose round on the way. Sent 300 behind itself, a Comanche turned 178 degrees before it went and was 30 out after a second and a half; now it is 30 out after one second with its nose already two thirds of the way round, and flies nose first a second and a half in. Players told us the first version of this flew backwards and sideways too much, so the nose now turns into every move longer than a helicopter's own length. A hop shorter than that is still flown sideways or backwards with the nose left alone, which is what you want when nudging one into place.
- A Comanche keeps its guns on what it is shooting at while it flies. Its cannon and missiles only fire straight ahead, so a Comanche ordered to move in the middle of a fight turned away from its target. It now holds its nose on the target and slides along beside it: one sent across in front of an Overlord it had been shooting stayed within a degree of the tank the whole way, where it used to point 84 degrees off on average. One flying past a supply truck 120 off its line now turns to it and shoots, where it used to fly straight by.
- The Comanche's chin gun turns, as the real one does, 120 degrees either side of the nose. EA bolted the cannon and the missiles straight ahead, so a Comanche had to swing its whole body round to shoot anything. Now the missiles still fly where the nose points, and the cannon tracks on its own: making a pass along four Red Guards, a Comanche turned 34 degrees where it used to turn 132, and its gun held within 4 degrees of the target while the nose stayed on the flight. A target behind the gun's reach turns the helicopter just far enough to bring it back in.
- Pull a helicopter out of a fight and it keeps shooting on the way out. Told to move while attacking, it used to drop the target at once; now it holds its gun on it for as long as the target is alive and in range, and the move goes exactly where and when it would have. A Comanche backs or slides away with its nose on the target: sent 350 straight back from three Battlemasters it fired 10 more rounds before they fell out of range, and 14 sliding off to the side, where it used to fire one. A plain move never starts a new fight; a helicopter that was not attacking anything just flies.
- Comanches fire their rocket pods sooner. Sent at a spot 800 away, a Comanche used to ease to a stop on the very edge of range before the first rocket left; now it flies in at full speed, fires the moment the spot comes into range and brakes under its own volley, then holds where it stopped. Braking that hard used to carry it past a standstill: it ran 57 past the edge of range and then backed 33 of that up again while it fired. Now it settles within one unit of where it first comes to rest. Any helicopter stopped hard at full speed holds its spot the same way. Four of them fired 6 seconds after the click, where they took 7.6 to 8.4. The rockets steer for the spot by themselves, so the helicopter no longer turns its nose to within 2 degrees of it first: a spot beside a hovering Comanche is hit 3 frames sooner, one behind it 3 frames sooner too. A Comanche whose pods are still reloading fires the moment they are full, as before. Replays recorded before this change, with a helicopter firing at the ground in them, will not play back the same.
- A Helix with the gattling cannon on its back lets the cannon do the aiming. The cannon sits on a turret that turns all the way round, yet the whole Helix used to swing round to point at whatever it was shooting. Now it holds its heading and only the turret tracks. With a Crusader driving circles round it, a Helix that used to turn 336 degrees following the tank does not turn at all, and its cannon fired 458 times where it fired 251. Infantry shooting out of a Helix bunker leave the Helix just as free once they are on its target. The Helix's own nose gun only fires forward, so while the cannon has the target it fires only when the target crosses in front.
- A helicopter leans into the move. It dips its nose to speed up and lifts it to stop, rolls into a slide sideways and comes level again in a hover, never past about 12 degrees and never with a jerk: the lean moves by at most a degree and a half from one frame to the next.
- Helicopters ease in and out of everything they do. Speed builds up over a moment instead of all at once, a stop flares out and settles on the exact spot it was sent to, and a turn winds up to full speed over a third of a second and winds down again before the nose arrives. Sent on a long flight behind itself, a helicopter sets off backwards at once and brings its nose round as it picks up speed; 30 out, the nose is still about 50 degrees off its course, where it used to have finished the whole turn before it got anywhere. Chinooks and Helixes fly the same way as the Comanche, and a gattling Helix keeps its gun on a tank while it slides past. A replay recorded before this change plays back differently.

## The frame rate cap is gone

- The picture now runs uncapped; the rules keep their own steady clock.
- On a machine with two graphics chips, the game used to draw on the first one it found, which was the slow one. It now uses the dedicated card, and a match holds about 260 frames a second with the trees, the shadows and the filtering left on.
- Display options has a vertical sync box. Off, the picture still runs free. On, it waits for the monitor so the frame does not tear. The box takes effect when you Accept.
- With vertical sync off, the picture really is free again. Ever since the game started drawing through Direct3D 11, fullscreen and windowed games were still held to the monitor's refresh: 164 frames a second on a 164 Hz screen, and a 60 Hz screen sat at 60 and sank into the fifties in a fight, where the old renderer had given players over 450. The same match now takes 2.3ms a frame, over 400 a second, where it was pinned at 6.1ms. Every picture is the same one it was. Vertical sync on still waits for the monitor.
- A slow moment costs you a dropped frame, not a slow game.
- Explosions no longer catch the picture. The first time each kind of fire, smoke or blast reached the screen, the game stopped for 25 to 60ms to prepare it, and the opening volley of a fight cost 300ms at once. That work is kept on disk and ships with the game, so it happens while the map loads, in about 3ms, and the worst frame of the same battle went from 141ms to 38ms.
- The price, build time and power figures on the command bar buttons stopped costing you frames. All the buttons shared one piece of text, so every figure on the bar was lettered from scratch every frame, about 33 of them. Each now keeps its own: a battle that averaged 14ms a frame averages 11.7ms, frames slower than 60 a second went from one in six to one in a hundred, and the old renderer gets about 0.8ms back too. The money readout was lettered again every frame as well, for a number that had not changed; it is lettered when the number moves. Over a whole battle the game now letters 163 pieces of text where it lettered 68,227.
- The seconds over a building that is producing, or over one counting down to a payout, were lettered from one shared line. Every building in view with a different time left had that line made again, every frame. Each building keeps its own, and it is made again only when the second changes. Twelve supply zones counting together, 1,200 frames: 313 letters, where the shared line wrote 6,181. The frame stayed at 8.6ms.
- The new command bar stopped eating half the frame. The clock and the frame counter in the top right corner sat inside the bar's own page, so every tick of them had the whole bar laid out again from scratch, 15ms a time and thirty times a second. The corner box is a page of its own now, the scoreboard counts again twice a second instead of every frame, and a click on the battlefield no longer lays the bar out twice. In the same four-player battle, almost half the frames used to miss 60 a second; now fewer than one in four hundred do, and the game draws more than twice as many frames. When the bar does have to be laid out again, on a hover, a click on it or the scoreboard's twice-a-second count, it takes 2ms where it took 14: the page engine was building an object for every word of the bar's style sheet and every space of its indenting, and reading the same style sheet from scratch each time. All ten pages were laid out both ways and every box and every word came out in the same place, size and colour.
- A screen full of fire and smoke is cheaper to draw. Thirty Inferno Cannons shelling twenty Crusaders used to send the graphics card 459 separate batches of see-through effects a frame, mostly the same texture again and again, and the game looked up the same seven card settings from scratch for every one of them. Matching effects go out together now, and settings that have not changed are not looked up again: 177 batches, and the same battle went from 11.6ms a frame to 8.6ms. It is at 6.7ms now, since the game stopped preparing everything it draws a second time for a copy of the picture nobody sees. Nothing on screen changes, the effects are drawn in exactly the order they were.
- The same battle with the particle limit turned up costs 7.6ms a frame where it cost 9.1ms, and the old renderer went from 9.3ms to 6.3ms. Both were keeping a count of every kind of draw the game makes, and both did it by writing out and reading back a description of each one, on every draw, for a report no player ever opens. They glance at one draw in sixteen now. The new renderer also stopped re-sending the graphics card settings it had just sent.
- The new renderer stopped redoing work between two pieces of ground that look the same. The map's terrain goes out as hundreds of tiles a frame under the same camera and the same settings, and for every tile the game worked the camera's arithmetic out again, rebuilt its description of the surface and of each texture's filtering, and compared the lot with the tile before. It now notices nothing was changed and goes straight to drawing. The fog of war is sent to the card only where it moved since the last frame, instead of all of it every frame. Two Hard computer players on Twilight Flame at 1920x1080 went from 2.46ms a frame to 2.24ms, and every picture is the one it was.
- The command bar and the observer's panel stopped reading their own pages from scratch every frame. The bar's page alone is 38 KB, and the game copied it, lowercased it and hunted through it for the blanks four times a frame before it wrote in a single number. Each page is read once now, and a frame only fills in the numbers. The same two computer players on Twilight Flame went from 2.03ms a frame to 1.84ms watched as an observer, and from 2.75ms to 2.50ms from a player's seat. Every page still comes out letter for letter as it did: two matches filled 270,000 of them both ways and found no difference.
- Ground painted with a third texture, the dirt over grass and the rock over sand, cost a trip to the memory manager for every corner of every such patch on screen, every frame, only to light that corner. Nearly a tenth of the game's time went to taking and releasing the memory manager's lock for it. Lighting a corner asks for nothing now: the same Twilight Flame match went from 1.88ms a frame to 1.71ms as an observer and from 2.56ms to 2.26ms from a player's seat, and eight views over five maps came out the same as before.
- The command bar stopped throwing its own layout away every frame. The few hundred positions and sizes it hands its page were written into a fresh list each frame, copied twice for the frames in front of the buttons, and thrown out, and every price and build time on a button was looked up by copying the text of every figure the bar was already showing. The list is written over in place now, nothing is copied, and the graphics card settings that did not change between two draws are not looked up again. The same Twilight Flame match from a player's seat went from 2.29ms a frame to 1.96ms, and as an observer from 1.64ms to 1.58ms. The picture is the same one.
- Raise the particle limit as far as you like and every puff gets drawn. Past about sixteen thousand smoke and fire sprites on screen the game quietly stopped drawing the rest: a test with 68,000 of them showed barely a fifth. A test with 101,000 now draws all of them at 8.3ms a frame, and in 1,200 frames not one fell below 60 a second. The first version that drew them all took 22.4ms; the rest came from sorting and copying them far less and from sharing the work across every core of the processor.
- Big fights no longer drag the picture down into the twenties. Every moving tank and soldier, and every puff of smoke, goes to the graphics card through one shared scratch area, and it was so small that a big fight on Bombardment Beach filled it about fifty times a frame. Each refill made the processor wait for the card, 8 to 12ms of every frame, while the card sat two thirds idle. The area is made full size once now and refills about seven times a frame, in 0.02ms. The same 16,000-frame battle, fast-forwarded, finishes in 226 seconds where it took between 263 and 325, and the seconds that fell under 50 frames a second went from 125 or more to 4.
- Crowded battles run a good deal faster. At 1080p, thirty Inferno Cannons against twenty Crusaders went from 153 to 227 frames a second, and six Brutal computer players on one map from 146 to 240. The slowest one frame in twenty got 22 to 34% shorter, and the slowest one in a hundred 16 to 29%. The game stopped re-sending lights, materials, textures and card settings that had not changed, stopped relighting the terrain when no light had moved, stopped adding up the glow of every fire particle twice, and widened low-colour textures with a lookup instead of arithmetic on each pixel. The new renderer keeps the old one's settings back until something actually reads them, re-sends only the half of each draw's settings that changed, and remembers each patch of ground's colours, its blend coordinates and its tread marks between frames. Units whose shadow cannot reach the screen are no longer drawn into the shadow map, except on maps with reflecting water. The command bar and the console replay what they drew last frame when nothing under them moved, and the HUD checks for changes without rebuilding its text. The rules side runs 4% quicker too: locking, the lists of nearby units, the sleeping-unit schedule and text strings all got cheaper. The picture matches the old build in all eight test views on both renderers, and a recorded match plays back to the same checksum.
- Every animation runs on a clock instead of counting frames.
- Tanks and trucks move the same at any frame rate. Their treads scrolled a fixed step every picture, so at 120 frames a second they ran four times too fast, and a tank turning on the spot rolled both treads forward as if it were driving. Each tread now covers exactly the ground under it. In a tight turn the two run against each other, and backing up runs them backwards. Wheels spin with the distance driven the same way. The hull's springs used to settle four times too fast as well, and every start or stop threw the chassis to its full tilt in a single picture. Now a tank squats as it pulls away and dips its nose when it brakes, easing in over a tenth of a second and settling within half a second. In a corner it leans out, a Crusader up to two and a half degrees. The Humvee, which never moved on its springs at all, rocks too. A run drawing one picture per game tick and one drawing fourteen put every tank in the same pose, to the last digit, for as long as neither skipped a tick.
- A scroll that starts after the camera has stood still no longer lurches. The first frame of the move was timed from the last time the camera moved, so after a few seconds of looking at one spot it was worth three frames of scrolling at once. The clock runs while the camera is still now, and the first frame moves one frame's worth.
- Briefing and cutscene subtitles hold long enough to read again.
- The radar's under-attack pulse throbs instead of strobing, and no longer ends early.
- The main menu surf rolls at its proper pace, not ten times too fast.
- And it is there at all again. The waves breaking on the main menu's beach had been worked out every frame and then drawn nowhere since v1.0.0, so the shore behind the menu was flat water.

## The computer opponent builds a base now

- One wrong value meant the AI built only urgent items and one power plant.
- Rotated AI bases were laid out wrong; buildings now face the right way.
- An AI with no buildings no longer aims everything at the map corner.
- The AI checks ten seconds of supply-line damage, not a third of one.
- One AI decision read leftover memory, so identical matches played out differently.
- Difficulty and AI money change build pace again; every order was pinned at three seconds.
- Computer bases no longer grow buildings inside buildings. When a spot in its plan was taken and it could find no free ground nearby, the computer put the building down on the taken spot anyway, straight through its own. It now looks outwards from the planned spot over ground its trucks could drive, bridges included, so a new building lands next to the base and not across a cliff or a river from it, and when there is truly no room it waits. It still stacked them on sloping ground, which a player caught. The check for what already stands on a spot is taken at a height, and the computer's search took it at the height of the spot it started from, or at zero, so wherever the ground fell away a building standing there was below the check and did not count. A Hard USA put three pairs of supply drop zones exactly on top of each other in one match. Every building is checked at the ground it will stand on now: over the same four four-player matches, the pairs stacked or half inside each other went from five to none.
- An urgent building it could not put up yet no longer stops the whole base. The computer always builds its most urgent request first, and when that request needed something it did not have, it built nothing else either: a Hard China that asked for a bunker before its barracks stood sat on 35,000 until well into the match. It now skips to the first urgent request it can actually build.
- A computer player no longer stands still with money in the bank. When its most urgent building had no room where it wanted it, it looked for room, found none, and looked again, and nothing else in its base went up in the meantime: one Hard GLA sat on 40,000 for most of a match because its Scud Storm did not fit. A request with no room is now dropped and asked for again somewhere else, and the rest of the base carries on. The check for room also no longer passes a spot on a hillside that the builder then refuses. Over eight Hard matches on Winter Wolf, the longest a base waited went from 18,000 frames to 5,400, and the time spent searching fell by six sevenths.
- Its supply trucks go back to work. When a pile ran dry, the trucks parked next to it for the rest of the match and the computer bought new ones instead: a Hard China kept nine trucks, eight of them idle. Idle trucks are now sent to a supply centre with boxes beside it, and a centre whose pile is gone gets no new trucks.
- Its base defences spread out. Every defence the computer asked for at a supply pile went up against the last one, and a Hard China stood seven or eight Gatling cannons shoulder to shoulder at one pile. It now puts at most two beside each pile.
- Replays recorded before this change play back differently.
- Hard USA opens the way a player does. It used to start on a barracks and hold its first power plant until a second dozer existed, so its first harvest came in at the 74 second mark. It now puts down the power plant and the supply centre straight away and the barracks after them. Over 20 Hard USA against Hard China matches on Tournament Desert, seats swapped both ways, USA won 15 where it won 10. The same opening was tried on China and lost it 17 matches instead of 10, and on GLA it decided too few matches to say anything, so both keep the opening they shipped with.
- The computer no longer shoots at what it cannot see. Its units, and its base defences, used to auto-target through the fog of war - a hard exemption written into the code for computer players only. Stealth works against it now, and so does anything it has not scouted.
- It scouts. There was no such thing as scouting in the computer opponent - it never needed to look, because it could already see everything. One cheap unit now tours the enemy start positions and keeps going round for the rest of the match, replaced out of spare change when it dies. Every difficulty scouts; an opponent that never looks reads as broken, not as easy. And it tours on purpose rather than in a circle: it goes to whichever base it has gone longest without seeing, nearest first, and it does not walk across the map to look again at something it looked at a minute ago - it stays and watches instead. Two scouts never trail each other round the same lap.
- It takes the oil derricks instead of blowing them up. An oil derrick pays whoever owns it and costs one infantryman to walk in - and the computer used to march past the neutral ones and shell yours, which is the one thing you can do with a derrick that earns nobody anything. It now keeps a cheap infantryman doing the rounds of every derrick, refinery and hospital it has found, takes them, and when there is nothing left to take it leaves him standing on the last one instead of walking him home. And it no longer picks a capturable building as a target of its own accord: it can still be ordered to level one, and one that shoots back is still shot back at. That infantryman never took anything before this, though. A Ranger, a Red Guard or a Rebel can only capture once the barracks has taught him how, and the computer never paid for the lesson, so he walked up to a derrick and stood on it for the whole match. It now buys the training the first time it sends him, and keeps him at home until it is done. Replays recorded before this change play out differently.
- It steals your tanks. A hijacker costs a few hundred and walks off with whatever it touches, and no computer opponent had ever bought one: not one shipped skirmish plan asks for it. A GLA computer now keeps a thief on call as soon as one of your vehicles comes within walking distance, and sends it at the nearest one rather than the richest, because the Humvee it reaches is worth more than the Overlord it dies halfway to. Black Lotus works the same ground on the same terms: she hacks a vehicle that is already in front of her, and does not cross the map to shut one down for a few seconds.
- Nor does it know which start position you took. It used to read that straight off the lobby, for every player, from the first second. Now it works it out: each position it has not looked at is a suspect, and the odds are simply how many opponents are still unaccounted for over how many places they could be - three of you on an eight-position map is 3 in 7 for each. Every empty position its scout crosses off makes the rest likelier, 3 in 6, then 3 in 5. When the numbers meet, it stops looking: three opponents with three places left to be is not a guess, and walking over to confirm it would waste the trip. On a two-player map that is true from the start, so nothing changed there - it never wastes a second hunting for an address it can work out by subtraction. Where it has not worked you out yet, it attacks the position you are most likely to be on, and finds out on arrival.
- Nor does it read your base off the map. Where your base is, what it is worth, which supply dock to expand to and where to aim a superweapon all came off a walk of your object list, in the shroud, from the first second of the match. The computer now only counts what it can see, plus the buildings it has already found - buildings do not walk away. Before it has scouted you, all it knows is where you started, which is on the map preview anyway.

## Superweapons that go where the damage is

- The computer picked its superweapon target by adding up what your things cost inside the blast, so a supply stash outranked the tank column parked next to it. Anything that can shoot back is now worth several times its price, and base defences and massed armour are what the shot goes looking for.
- It could only aim inside the rectangle your buildings sit in. An attack wave sitting on its doorstep was never a target at all; now your army counts wherever it is standing.
- Two silos ready on the same second fired at the same spot, because nothing that was already in the air was on the map yet. A spot that has just been aimed at is worth nothing to the next shot and climbs back over ten minutes, so the salvo spreads across your base instead of digging one crater.
- Measured over a half-hour match: a Particle Cannon that fired at the identical spot three times running now spreads six shots over five parts of the map, and comes back to the first one at the end, when what you rebuilt there is worth twice what it killed.
- The spy satellite goes through the same targeting, and it was scanning one cell of one base seventeen times a match. It sweeps now.

## Generals powers the computer actually meant to buy

- It spent every promotion point the moment it had one, on whatever in its list happened to be cheap enough. The strong three-point ability at the end of the list was never reached, because the points were always already gone.
- From Medium up it saves. If the next thing in its own list is only out of reach on points, it waits for them instead of buying filler.
- Which list it draws from now follows its personality, so the powers coming at you tell you which kind of opponent you are facing.

## The computer comes in where you are thin

- The game has kept a value-and-danger map of every square of the battlefield, per player, since 2003. It has a query interface and even a debug view. Nothing in the computer opponent had ever read it - its only users were two map-script actions almost nobody used.
- At the top level the opponent now aims its attacks at where your money is rather than at the middle of your buildings, which is also the middle of your defences.
- It reads that map through the fog like everything else: only ground it has actually seen counts. An opponent that has not scouted you still attacks the old way.

## The computer expands, and defends what it takes

- It never decided to expand. The machinery was all there - place a supply centre beside a pile, pick a pile worth taking, send a team to sit on it - and every piece of it only ran when a map script said so. An opponent that ran its starting piles dry simply stopped earning.
- It now goes and takes the money that is lying around, out of spare cash so the army never pays for it.
- On Hard the expansion comes with a defence structure facing the enemy, placed in the same job. An undefended expansion is a gift.
- Measured: a third more army in the field and a quarter more money spent over the match.
- With Share Income set to All Income it leaves its allies' piles alone. Every payment is already split across the team, so a computer that put a supply centre beside its ally's pile added nothing and emptied that pile twice as fast. It now expands only to piles nobody on its side is working. Over eight 2v2 matches on Twilight Flame, loads taken from an ally's pile went from 396 to 54 and the team's income stayed where it was. Replays recorded before this change play out differently.
- It leaves a pile alone when someone else is already working it. The computer only checked for its own supply centre beside a pile, so a pile inside an ally's base, or a third player's, looked free, and it walked a supply centre in next to theirs with a Patriot or a Gatling cannon after it. It now skips any pile with another side's building beside it, friend or foe. Over eight 2v2 matches on Twilight Flame, buildings it put down at a pile someone else held went from 116 to 2, and over eight four-way matches from 8 to 4, with spending where it was. Replays recorded before this change play out differently.
- It keeps its money-makers and its superweapons at home. A Hard computer with a full base looked for room further and further out until the map ended, and a black market or a Scud Storm could go up alone on ground nobody held. Those buildings, and the tech building a superweapon waits on, now go inside the base or at its edge, and when there is no room they are not built: the money goes into another factory. Expansions are held to the same standard: a supply centre only goes to a pile on its own side of the map and within reach of home, never to one with an enemy standing at it, and the next expansion waits until the last one has a gun or a guard beside it. Replays recorded before this change play out differently.

## The computer spends its money

- It no longer sits on a pile of cash. Past a level that depends on the difficulty, the more money it has the faster it builds - twice the pile, half the wait, and it stops at four times. It is spending sooner, not building faster: the rate itself is untouched.
- A full bank buys a bigger army. Late in a match the computer's plan allows one of each attack group, so once they were all on the map it only replaced its losses and banked the rest: a Hard USA sat on 117,000 on Twilight Flame. Now every 8,000 in the bank on Hard allows one more copy of each attack group, up to four of them.
- Hard builds past its plan. A tank factory or a barracks gets another copy when units are waiting in its queue, and a factory that was destroyed is put back. Airfields keep going up for as long as the money is there. Supply drop zones and black markets go up one after another as soon as the bank is past a small cushion, whatever else it is buying; they used to wait until production had nothing to ask for, which on a busy map was never, and over twelve four-player matches the count went from 5 to 95. Internet centers follow that same rule. The spot search used to give up two rings outside the opening base, so a full base simply stopped. It now keeps looking further out until the map ends, for factories and power plants; the income buildings stay at home. Strategy centers, propaganda centers and palaces stop at three, so one of them blowing up leaves the tech tree standing. Extra factories still go beside the newest expansion it holds, nearer the fighting. China trains a hacker from every barracks that has room in its queue, not one barracks at a time: 120 hackers across those matches became 375. A hacker left standing still is put to work. It stops at six, an internet center's four plus a couple, because nothing counted them before: a Hard Tank General trained 67 over twenty thousand frames, about two thirds of everything it spent, and every one of them went through the barracks queue its army was waiting in. A player watching it from the other side of the map said China's tank general builds nothing but infantry, and he was right. Seven now, and the teams at the top of its build list are tanks. The limit grows with the match: one hacker for every four fighting units, never more than its Internet Centers have seats for plus four working outside, and never fewer than four. A computer that has not built an army does not buy an economy instead, and one with a full Internet Center and ninety units behind it keeps eight. Replays recorded before this change play out differently.
- Hard picks its road into your base. Of the three approaches it takes the one with the fewest guns it has seen along it, and keeps to the one its plan named until it has scouted something. Aiming at your money instead was tried first and walked it straight past your army.
- Hard sends its attack groups out together. A group too small to be a wave on its own waits on the near side of its base until about seven tanks' worth has gathered, or a minute and a half has passed, and then everything waiting leaves on one road at the pace of its slowest unit. A lone artillery piece or a single bomb truck used to drive across the map by itself, and nearly a third of Hard's units died with barely anyone of their own nearby.
- It reads the odds before it attacks. Every attack was sized the same way whoever it was aimed at, so a computer with an army at home went on waiting for a full wave while the enemy across the map had nothing left to fight with. It now weighs its own army against what it has seen of yours, and an army it cannot see it assumes is growing back towards the size of its own. It has to be looking at your buildings before it believes it is ahead. Ahead by about two to one with your base in view, Hard stops waiting and sends each group as it forms. Ahead by about six to one, the computer on any difficulty sends the guards it used to keep at home for the whole match hunting as well, half a minute apart. Outnumbered two to one, Hard and Medium keep their groups at home until twice the usual wave has gathered, and a handful is never sent into an army that size. Two leaks went with it. Units that reached the end of their road into your base used to stop there and wait for something to shoot at them; they now go looking. A group that got reinforcements at home was ordered out again as a whole, which marched the half already fighting in your base back to the middle of the map to start the walk over. Only the ones at home leave now. Over sixteen Hard against Hard matches on Twilight Flame stopped at seventeen minutes, the five that finished took 12 minutes 26 seconds on average where they had taken 13 minutes 55. The other eleven still ran to the stop: the side that is ahead kills units faster than buildings, and a defender with 120,000 in the bank replaces them at its own door. Replays recorded before this change play out differently.
- Hard stops piling its army up at home. Its plan only sends an attack group while the signal for the next wave is up, and only a group standing in the base at that moment. A group finished a minute later guarded the base until the signal came round again, and one that fell back from a lost fight guarded it for good: one GLA stood 38 of its Angry Mob by its own buildings with 70,000 in the bank. China's Dragon Tank group never left at all, because the attack order its plan names for it is missing from EA's file. Now any attack group standing idle at home joins the next wave. Rich computers also stopped building extra copies of the groups that only ever guard, four sets of tunnel guards where the plan asked for one. Over eight Hard matches on Twilight Flame, the units standing idle at home late in the match went from 43 to 17, the waves sent went from 134 to 319, and kills went up by a tenth. Replays recorded before this change play out differently.
- The defenders it trains in a hurry go on to fight. Units a Hard computer bought while its base was being hit belonged to no attack group, so once that fight was over they stood wherever it had left them for the rest of the match. They now leave with the next attack, and any of them stuck out on the map goes hunting. An infantryman sent to take an oil derrick or a tech building could also stop halfway and stand frozen at the door for minutes on end; he finishes the job now. Over sixteen Hard matches on Twilight Flame, the computer's fighting units that had not moved or fired for half a minute went from 5.9 a side to 3.6, and it sent 1,155 attack waves where it had sent 1,079. Replays recorded before this change play out differently.
- It puts more harvesters on a supply centre with more piles around it, instead of the same three numbers everywhere.
- USA's Chinooks stop collecting over the command center. When the pile beside a supply centre ran out, its Chinooks flew home and hovered, and the computer went on buying new ones for its other centres while those sat idle. It only looked for an idle gatherer when a pile stood right next to the centre, a fraction of how far a Chinook looks. An idle gatherer is now sent to any of its supply centres with a pile inside that gatherer's own range, and nothing new is bought while one stands idle beside a pile that has no room for it.
- It keeps its lights on, and keeps them out of reach. Its plan carries a fixed number of power plants and nothing ever adds one, so a raid that took two of them left the base underpowered - radar dark, base defences off, superweapon clock stopped - until a rebuild timer came round and put the new plant back on the spot the raid already knew the way to. It now buys a plant when the margin gets thin rather than when the meter reads zero, and puts it on the far side of the base from whoever it is fighting.
- Spare cash also buys a gun on the side the trouble comes from. Production and income go up around the middle of the base, the power goes behind it, and base defences go out in front, so money that keeps arriving thickens the base in the order a base wants to be thick in. Front means towards the nearest enemy, which is not the same as the one it has decided to attack - that one can be across the map, and a wall built facing it has its back to the neighbour. The walls grow with the army. One gun every minute and a half, never more than ten, used to be the rule; now the computer may stand one gun for every four fighting units it has, so a big army comes home to a fortress and a beaten one rebuilds its tanks before its bunkers. Letting it buy guns on every pass instead was tried first: 241 guns over twelve four-player matches, and they ate the army, down to 60 kills a match from 73. Tied to the army, 71 guns, and the army is the biggest of any version tried, 78 kills a match. A small army no longer means a bare base, though: one gun for every three fighting units, and however small the army, one more every 30 seconds of the match up to 24, so the twelfth is allowed six minutes in. The computer buys them as soon as its bank clears its saving threshold rather than twice that, and a base three or more guns short of its allowance orders two at a time.
- Each gun goes where it sees the most of the way in. The computer used to aim one spot at the enemy's side of the base and, when that was taken, put the gun down wherever it fitted, which on a crowded map meant behind its own factories with nothing to shoot at. Now it looks at nearly a hundred spots around the base and scores each one by the open ground inside the gun's reach that lies between the base and the enemy. Ground hidden behind its own buildings or a cliff counts for nothing, ground at the back of the base counts for very little, and every gun already standing nearby halves the score, so the guns spread along the front instead of piling up on one corner. It only asks the build rules about the best dozen of those spots, and a spot under a building, on unbuildable ground or within reach of the enemy's own base is crossed off first, so the gun usually goes on the first spot it asks about: 296 of 349 did over 72 matches.
- Its guns come as a mix. It used to put up whichever defence came first on the worker's build bar, every time: a Hard USA base put up 22 Patriots for every Fire Base, a GLA one Stinger Sites, and China's own spare-cash guns were bunkers. Now each new gun is the type the base has fewest of, so Patriots and Fire Bases go up in turn, Stinger Sites alternate with Tunnel Networks, and China's Gattling Cannons get bunkers and Speaker Towers beside them. Those two shoot at nothing on their own, so the computer keeps about one of each for every three cannons. A type lost in a raid is the one rebuilt first. Every general's own versions count, and the planned defences its build scripts ask for follow the same rule. Over 48 one-on-one matches between Hard and Normal computers and 24 between two Hards, the same seeds each time, a Hard USA side built 4.9 guns a match where it built 2.4, 1.6 of them Fire Bases where it built 0.1. China went from 4.0 to 9.9 and GLA from 4.9 to 6.9. Hard won 29 of those 48 matches against Normal, where it won 25. Replays recorded before this change play out differently.
- Hard builds its superweapon as soon as it can pay for one and has the guns to stand it on, and then another. Its scripts held the first one back behind a clock that ran for twelve and a half minutes, seven and a half if you had built your own tech building first, and then allowed exactly one: a player who took on seven Hard computers at once saw a single nuke all match. There is no clock now. The tech building that unlocks it goes up first, and the superweapon follows once the money is in the bank and twelve of its guns stand finished, a rule only the computer plays by (see the lobby section). More follow as the base grows, one for every twelve guns, so a computer holding twenty-four guns can stand two superweapons. The numbers below were measured when four guns were enough. With twelve, a one-on-one between computers rarely lasts long enough: over 96 Hard sides in the same 72 matches, 11 had a superweapon up with four guns and 7 with twelve, the first at 5.4 minutes and most between 8 and 9. Before that rule it put its first particle cannon up at 4.3 minutes on Twilight Flame and its first nuke at 4.9, without a gun to its name. Over 24 matches between two Hard computers on the same map, the first nuke now goes up at 6.2 minutes as a rule, anywhere from 4.4 to 9.8, and the first Particle Cannon at 7.4. The Scud Storm comes last at 7.6 but never later than 8.7, and every GLA side had one inside ten minutes, where 4 of the 32 China and USA sides were still waiting for theirs. The lobby's superweapon limit and Pro Rules still apply to the computer exactly as they do to you.
- Hard trains more builders when it has work for them. It only ever made a new dozer after losing all of them, so it played every match on the two it had, and every building it bought queued behind those two. On Twilight Flame a third of the China Tank computers never got round to a war factory, never sent an attack, and ended up sitting in a ring of bunkers on a pile of cash. Now, with money in the bank and every dozer busy, it trains another, up to four. Over six four-player matches on that map, every computer built its war factories, the attack waves went from 39 to 82, and each one spent half as much again.
- Hard no longer rebuilds its factories inside your base. A busy computer puts its next war factory beside its newest expansion, and it remembered that expansion forever: after you destroyed the supply centre there, even one it had built right next to your base, it kept putting the factory back up on the same spot. It now only builds beside an expansion it still holds, and only one nearer its own base than yours.
- Hard keeps its lights on. It asked for a new power plant before running out, and the request then sat in a queue behind everything else its plan wanted: one China waited 95 seconds for a plant with 26,000 in the bank, every builder busy, and went dark halfway. A plant now jumps that queue once the margin is thin, a busy builder is taken off whatever it is doing to put it up, and while the base is dark the computer buys nothing else. Over eight four-player matches the time its bases spent without power fell by a third, from 42 minutes to 28 across all of them.
- Its hackers work from home. A hacker set to work wherever it stood, which was the barracks' rally point, so China's income sat at the front of its base. It now goes into an Internet Center with room, or walks to the quiet side of the base first.
- Its helicopters fight. The computer's plan parks every Comanche and Helix on guard, and there they stayed for the whole match. Now when an attack leaves, every helicopter left on guard goes with it and flies over the army at its pace: 97 of them over eight four-player matches, where none had ever left before. A Helix buys its own upgrades, and infantry waiting for the attack climbs into any helicopter it can shoot out of.
- And its helicopters carry people. On Hard, infantry waiting for the attack now boards any helicopter with a seat, not only one it can shoot out of, and a helicopter carrying riders who cannot shoot from it puts them down just short of the fight, on the first ground none of your guns it knows about can reach, from where they go on at you on foot. The boarding never worked before this either: a helicopter the plan had put on guard stayed in the air, so the riders walked up to it and turned away. The same Black Lotus was sent to one Helix three times in two minutes. A helicopter now lands for its riders. The capturer flies too: when the building it is after is a long walk away and a helicopter is waiting at home, it gets in, and the helicopter puts it down beside the building. China's attacks are mostly tanks, so its Helixes still leave with one rider more often than five.
- Its infantry rides in its vehicles. The computer's plan tells a group with a Humvee to climb in and, in the same breath, to stand guard, and the guard order won before anyone took a step: over two Hard four-player matches not one soldier boarded anything, Humvee, Battle Bus or helicopter. Now soldiers standing at home climb into their own group's Humvee or Battle Bus on every difficulty, and the group goes wherever its plan sends it with them shooting from inside. Soldiers waiting for the next attack fill any Humvee, Battle Bus or helicopter at home they can shoot out of, an Overlord or Emperor carrying its battle bunker included, and that vehicle leaves with the attack. A soldier who could not shoot from a vehicle never rides in one, except in a helicopter on Hard, which puts him down short of the fight as before. China's attacks on Hard carry no infantry at all, so its Helixes flew off with the waves empty, 14 times over those two matches, though both had bought the bunker by the middle of the match. Medium and Hard now train soldiers for any helicopter seat they could shoot from that stands empty, an attack ready to leave waits up to twenty seconds for them to climb in, and an empty bunkered Helix whose soldiers are still on their way stays behind for the next attack rather than fly off unarmed. The Air Force General on Medium and Hard also buys up to two Combat Chinooks, where it only ever bought the supply Chinook before. They stay off the supply runs and fill with Rangers. On Hard they fly with the attacks, all eight shooting from inside; Medium mostly keeps them at home, loaded. Replays recorded before this change play out differently.
- Its buildings have men in them. The computer built Bunkers and Fire Bases, put up its Palace as GLA, and left every one of them empty for the whole match. Now the soldiers guarding its base and the ones standing idle at home climb into its own buildings they can shoot out of, and on Medium and Hard it trains more for the empty seats once it has money to spare. While its base is being hit, the men at home also take any empty civilian building in the base. The soldiers gathered for an attack are never taken off it for this. Over eight Hard matches on Twilight Flame, 173 soldiers went in, each of them once, 88 of them into a Palace and the rest into Fire Bases and Bunkers, with the matches' economy and number of attacks where they were. A first version bought five Red Guards for China's first Bunker a minute into the match, and the computer's own plan, finding the bank empty, sold the whole base in two of eight matches; it now waits for spare money. Replays recorded before this change play out differently.
- Its Chinooks fly the army. A USA attack walked the whole road, at the pace of its slowest tank. Medium and Hard now buy plain Chinooks for nothing but lift, more of them the bigger the attack and the longer the road: they load tanks and soldiers at home, put them down on the attack's road short of anything the computer has seen that can shoot, and go home for the next load while the attack is under way. The drop is one a straight flight from the base reaches over quiet ground, after the first version flew seven loaded Chinooks in one match straight at a drop beyond an enemy base and lost every one. A Chinook hit hard on the way puts its load down where it is. Neither these nor the Air Force General's Combat Chinooks go on the supply runs unless nothing else is left to gather and nothing else can be bought, and the Combat Chinook is no longer ever bought as a gatherer.
- Its Helixes have jobs. Every Helix the computer owned bought its upgrades in button order, so each one carried a battle bunker whatever it was up against, with napalm on top. A Helix in one of its attack or guard groups now looks at what it knows of your army first and takes the gattling cannon when most of your strength is on foot, the bunker when most of it is in vehicles, and the soldiers trained to sit in that bunker are Tank Hunters rather than Red Guards. Against GLA over four Hard matches that came out 15 bunkers to 6 cannons, each decided on the split at that moment. China on Medium and Hard also buys two Helixes as healers, a third once its army passes thirty units, each under a Propaganda Tower. They hang behind the attack over whichever of its units is most hurt, carry nobody, and pull away when they are hit or when anti-air they know of comes near; over twelve Hard matches they spent 93% of their time out of the base further from the nearest enemy gun than it could shoot. They used to bunch up over the same tank, which got nothing out of it: a unit inside two towers heals no faster than inside one. Each healer now keeps its spot at least a tower's reach, 150, from the others', going to the most hurt unit nobody else's tower already covers, or stepping sideways behind the attack until it is clear of theirs. Two more Helixes take the napalm bomb, or the Nuke General's nuclear one, and raid your base, a factory first and otherwise whatever pays for it or powers it. A raid comes in from the side your known anti-air does not cover, looks for a new way in when a gun turns up ahead, and turns for home when it is hit below half health. Over the same twelve matches 126 raids went out, 23 dropped their bomb, five buildings fell after a drop, and 31 raiders were shot down on a raid. A raider also stops bombing once its run is over. A Helix told to bomb a spot drops another bomb under itself every ten seconds until it gets a different order, and the computer's own orders never counted as one, so a raider that had bombed your base went on dropping napalm all the way home, over your soldiers on the way, and then on its own base for the rest of the match. Over eight Hard matches against America, 104 of 121 bombs fell nowhere near the building they were meant for. On the same matches now, each of the 14 bombs dropped lands on its building, and a raid that turns back before its drop no longer flies out again to finish it once it is home. The Infantry General's Helix has no tower, so it only raids. Replays recorded before this change play out differently.
- The USA computer fields Ambulances. It never built one before. On Medium and Hard it buys one once eight fighting units stand and a second at twenty, and keeps them a little behind the middle of its army, short of every gun of yours it knows about. A soldier of its own under half health who stands near one walks over and climbs in, and gets out again once he is whole. Toxins, anthrax and radiation on its own side of the map get driven to and washed off. Over six Hard matches against GLA on Twilight Flame it ordered 26 Ambulances, called 10 wounded men in and sent them on 19 cleanups of its own ground. They also die a lot: 24 of the 25 that rolled out were lost, and those six matches went no better for the USA than the same six without them (no win either way, 57 kills a match where it had 66). Replays recorded before this change play out differently.
- It comes at you from two sides. An attack big enough to be two sends a third of its strength down the second-quietest road, so you cannot meet the whole thing in one place.
- It holds ground between attacks. Hard gathers its next wave a third of the way along the road to you, not in front of its own base, as long as nothing it has seen can shoot at that spot; otherwise it gathers at home.

## The computer picks its fights
- Which enemy it goes after was the nearest one and nothing else, plus a rule with its sign the wrong way round: an opponent who had lost his units or his production had his distance treated as half the map, so the computer ignored the one it was about to beat. That is what dragged matches out.
- It now weighs distance, whether the target is crippled - an opportunity, not a distraction - and how much of what it can see that player is worth. Only a genuinely finished enemy is skipped.
- It still refuses to gang up with another computer opponent on one victim, and still gently prefers whoever is already shooting at it.
- An aggressive computer team is supposed to fight its way to where it is sent rather than walk there. It never did. The order to fight was being reissued every frame, and each reissue pushed the moment that unit next looks for a target one step further into the future, so the look never came at all and the team crossed the map through the enemy without firing. Over four and a half minutes of one test match that order was restarted 2,240 times. It is restarted 15 times now.
- And when that switch does happen, the old order stops there. It used to finish its own turn afterwards, steering one last time toward the place the unit was going before the order changed, which is a visible flinch at the head of a column.
- It no longer chases what it cannot see. A stealthed Colonel Burton nobody had detected still counted as seen to the computer, so walking him into its base drew the whole guard after him while not one of their guns was allowed to fire at him. A hidden unit is now hidden from the computer the way it is from you, until a detector or a shot of his own gives him away, and a GLA bomb truck dressed as one of the computer's own units fools it as well. In four staged runs a Burton pacing inside a USA base had the guard sent at him about 70 seconds in every time; now nothing goes for him until the base's first Patriot Battery, which can see him, is up.
- Hard stops feeding you units. Before a group leaves, or a lone reinforcement sets off to join one out on the map, it weighs what it is sending against everything of yours it has seen in the last three minutes, unit type against unit type, and holds back what would only die. A reinforcement waits at home for the next wave instead of crossing the map alone. When you mass one unit it trains the answer between attacks too, Comanches and Helixes included, and stops buying what your biggest group beats. Against waves of Overlords rolling at its base, the share of its groups sent into a fight their units lose fell from 48% to 40%, and units dying alone from 17% to 14% of its losses. Against itself it changed nothing measurable: in 48 matches where only one side had it, that side won 5 and the other 4. Replays recorded before this change play out differently.
## The computer knows when to quit

- Its teams fought to the last man. The word "retreat" appeared nowhere in the opponent's code - the single most visible thing that made it look stupid.
- It now measures the fight, not the health bar: how long its force lasts against how long it needs to finish yours. A unit at a fifth of its health that still out-damages what is shooting it stays; a full-health one being melted leaves. A health percentage gets both of those backwards.
- Two levels of it. On Medium a unit that is personally finished pulls out of a fight its team is still winning. On Hard the whole team breaks off when the exchange is lost. Easy never quits - that is part of what makes it easy.
- It only counts what it can see. An opponent that flinches from something it has not found is reading your unit list again.
- It weighs a fight by what is in it, not by who is nearby: only things that can shoot count, and buildings do not. Its own base is not a reason to feel safe and yours is not a reason to run.
- Its aircraft are left out of it. A retreat order was what took a parked jet off the runway, so while a fight raged near the airfield the whole wing took off, flew at the base, landed, and did it again every few seconds instead of ever reaching you. Aircraft already fly home on their own when the load is spent.
- Matches finish. Two of these opponents used to fail to settle 65% of their games inside sixteen minutes; it is half that now, and they end in under seven minutes on average instead of ten.
- The retreat actually happens now, on Normal as well as Hard. The order home was handed to units that were already fighting, and the game laid it over their attack instead of replacing it, so a team told to break off carried on driving at what it was shooting, and its aggressive mood turned any walk that did start into a fight on the way. A unit that makes it home gets its mood back when it stops walking.
- A beaten team stops running once it is out of range. It used to break off a fight at your front door and walk the whole map back to its base at full health, where nothing repaired it, then stand there until a script or the next wave remembered it. Now it falls back just past the reach of the guns it was losing to, waits, and goes straight back in as soon as that fight turns: your units leave or die, or enough of its own arrive. If the fight still looks lost half a minute later, it goes home and joins the next wave. A team only walks home first when your guns cover the whole road. In 32 Hard against Hard matches on Twilight Flame, 2,748 of 2,756 retreats stopped short of home, and the side doing it lost 92 units a match where it had lost 96, for the same number of kills; that difference is inside the noise of 32 matches. Replays recorded before this change play out differently.
- It looks after its veterans. A heroic tank cost the computer no more thought than one off the line, and it fought to the last chevron. Now a unit with a rank pulls out on its own health, whatever its team does: under 35% for a veteran, 45% for an elite, 55% for a heroic. It also leaves a fight its team is losing before the team gives up, the higher the rank the sooner, though never one it is even in. It goes to whatever of its side's buildings can mend it, a tank to its repair point or a soldier to his barracks, and home when there is none. The computer also counts a ranked unit for a quarter more per chevron when it weighs one army against another, yours included, since your chevrons are on show. Over eight Hard matches on Twilight Flame it pulled out 422 ranked units, 28 of them heroic, and 358 of those went to a building that mends them. Both sides lost 1,979 units where the same matches had cost them 2,080, and 3% less in value, with the same economy. Replays recorded before this change play out differently.
