  anyone could react. Soldiers with the same weapon now take turns across their reload: the Humvee still
  fires five rockets a reload, one after another. Rifles keep their own turns and never wait behind the
  rockets.

## A defence earns its price

- A tower used to be cheaper to kill than to build. We put every stock defence against 22 kinds of attacker, 528 fights a round, and in Zero Hour as shipped two Battlemasters took a Gattling Cannon and lost nothing, and tanks paid 89 cents in wrecks for every dollar of Patriot they destroyed. Tank shells now do half damage to the Patriot, the Gattling Cannon, the Stinger Site and the Fire Base. Taking one with tanks now costs you more than the tower cost them: a Patriot $1.23 in tanks per dollar, a Stinger Site $1.09, a Fire Base $1.26.
- The Gattling Cannon costs 1000, the same as a Patriot. It is still the tower for infantry and aircraft, and it makes a rifle rush pay $1.72 for every dollar of it.
- Bunkers and Tunnel Networks stand half as long again, 1500 instead of 1000. One of them in a game saved before this change comes back at full health when you load it.
- Heavy artillery is untouched. A Tomahawk, an Inferno Cannon, a Nuke Cannon or a Scud Launcher outranges every tower in the game and still takes one without losing a thing. Bring them when a player digs in.
- The Rocket Buggy fires from 235 instead of 300. Its rockets used to outreach every tower by 75, so a pack of buggies took a Patriot or a Bunker without a scratch. They still take it most of the time, and now leave wrecks worth a fifth to four fifths of what the tower cost.
- A Paladin's laser fires every two seconds instead of every second. It used to shoot down every missile a Patriot sent at it, so four Paladins killed a Patriot every time and lost a quarter of its price doing it, while Crusaders for the same money never broke one. The Patriot now stands half the time.
- The Quad Cannon costs 800, up from 700 (850 for the Toxin and Demolitions generals). For the same money seven of them were an even fight for five Crusaders; now tanks beat them, and they still shred every kind of infantry.
- The Dragon Tank has 400 health instead of 280. It was the worst unit in the game for its price and lost to every armored target; now it trades evenly with Paladins and Quad Cannons and still loses to main battle tanks.
- Replays recorded before this change play out differently.

## Weapons and units that were quietly wrong

- The Dragon Tank's Fire Wall burns where you put it again. An earlier change in this build handed each patch of the wall the full speed of the flame that lit it, so the fire slid off across the map: in one test the patches came to rest anywhere from 155 to 1,050 away from the tank, 800 on average, setting fire to whatever stood in the way. They now stop 120 to 180 from the tank, a little past where the flames land, as they did in the original game. Napalm bombs and the wrecks of shot-down planes and drones keep their original drift too. Replays recorded before this change play out differently.
- A bunker buster shot down on the way in no longer clears out the building it was aimed at. It emptied the bunker from wherever it happened to be destroyed - a Point Defence Laser did not save you, it just moved the explosion.
- A Battle Bus in its wrecked form takes attack orders again. Every order you gave it was dropped on the way, because the crew inside are 'held' and held units were skipped.
- A sniper cannot pick at an empty Stinger Site. The rule that stops snipers hurting empty buildings only counted passengers, and a Stinger Site's men are spawned, not carried.
- A booby trap shot off a building, or sold with it, releases the building. The mark was only ever cleared when the trap went off, so the building stayed marked for the rest of the match and no second trap could be laid.
- Something flagged unselectable is unselectable. The flag existed and nothing read it.
- A suicide unit pressed up against a building goes off. Touching the building's outline counted as having its view blocked, so it walked away to find a clear line of sight it never needed, came back, and did it again without ever detonating.
- A nuclear missile hurts the whole of a big building. The blast measured to the middle of everything near it, so an airfield whose centre sat just outside the outer ring took nothing while half of it stood in the fire. Distance is now taken halfway between the nearest edge and the centre.
- Rockets curve into their target instead of snapping onto it. In the last few frames before impact a guided missile swung its nose straight at the target in one step, up to 40 degrees in a single frame, when the whole flight before that never turned more than 5. It now flies an arc from the heading it had to the spot where it meets the target, so a tank driving across its path is led rather than chased. In the same test battle no rocket turns more than 8 degrees in a frame, and all 101 land. Rockets fired at aircraft still fly straight at them: a helicopter is nearly as fast as the rocket, and on an arc the rocket flew past it and climbed away into the sky. A rocket whose target is behind it flies straight at it too. The arc to something behind the nose loops out past it, and when the target is on the ground under a helicopter the bottom of that loop is in the dirt: a Comanche hovering over a supply truck put all four missiles into the ground 23 to 47 away from it, while the same Comanche 250 away landed all four. Now the truck is down to 16 health of 300 five seconds in, where it had 136. Rockets fired at driving tanks and at aircraft land exactly where they did before. A rocket fired at a soldier on the move now goes where he will be when it gets there. Rockets at infantry fly at a spot on the ground rather than at the man, and a soldier walking across the line of fire had left that spot before the rocket came down. A Stinger Site shooting at twelve Rangers walking past it killed three in each of six matches where it used to kill one, and took 627 health off the squad where it took 447. A rocket at someone standing still lands where it always did. A replay recorded before this change, with rockets fired at moving infantry, will not play back the same.
- Scuds come down from the top of their climb in a smooth curve, level at the top and steepest at the end, instead of tipping into a straight dive 200 short of the target. Over those last 200 a Scud now stays within 14 of that curve where the dive strayed by up to 49, and every one lands on the spot it was fired at; one in the old test came down 22 away.
- Cancelling an order of Red Guard after the first soldier has walked out no longer refunds the order. One order is two soldiers for one price, and the refund came back whole, so the first one was free.
- Double-clicking to grab everything of a kind grabs what a box would, not things you can only click one at a time.
- A bounty pays what the percentage says. Rounding up a fraction that was already a whole number paid a dollar extra on every kill.
- A supply upgrade pays for what arrives. The bonus was a flat sum handed over on arrival, so a truck turning up with one box collected the same as a full load - and a driver dropping off little and often earned several times what the upgrade is worth.
- Taking over a defeated ally's base no longer starts his research again from scratch. A player upgrade is bought once for the whole player, and the buildings you inherit were left paying for a second copy of what you already had.
- A tank drives out the moment it is paid for. The factory doors only began opening once the vehicle was already finished, and it stood inside waiting for them, so every single unit cost its build time plus the whole door animation on top - on every tank, all game. The doors now start moving during the last seconds of the build and are open on the frame the unit is done. And they stay open while there is another vehicle behind him, however long that takes: a factory working through a queue keeps its doors up and shuts them when the queue runs out, instead of dropping and hauling them back for every unit. That was worth watching in its own right - there is no artwork for a door that changes its mind partway, so a door caught mid-close and pulled back open snapped wide in a single frame.
- A unit lifts the fog only half as far again as its guns reach: enough to spot what it is about to fight, not enough to scout. A rifleman used to see well past the range of his rifle, so any cheap soldier was a scout. Artillery that outshoots its own eyes still needs a spotter, and units with no weapon at all, like bulldozers and supply trucks, keep the sight they had. Buildings are unchanged.
- Workers and bulldozers can see again. Each one carries a mine-clearing tool a few feet long, and the rule above took it for a gun, so a builder saw no further than the end of its own blade and walked into the fog blind. They keep their full sight now, armed or not.
- Hills and buildings block sight. A tank at the foot of a ridge used to see straight through it to the far side, and a squad in a city saw through every block around it. Now the fog stays on whatever a ridge, a building or a tree hides, yours, the enemy's or a civilian's, and a unit on high ground looks down over everything below it. Aircraft fly above it all and see past. Soldiers garrisoned in a building still see out of it; its own walls do not blind them.
- A bridge is ground you can see, not a wall. Sight judged a bridge by the valley floor under it, so a bridge over a gorge sat hidden behind the gorge's own edge and a tank on the bank could not see the deck it was about to cross. A standing deck counts at its own height now; a bridge knocked down to rubble goes back to the ground beneath it.
- The spy satellite, the radar van scan and the spy drone see everything in their circle again. They were blocked by hills and buildings like a tank on the ground, so a scan dropped on an enemy base came back with holes behind every building in it.
- High ground pays. A tank on a ridge now shoots, sees and picks its targets further down the slope than it could on the flat: three steps of range for every step of height over its target, up to twice its usual reach. Firing uphill costs nothing, and aircraft get no bonus from flying. Defences count too, and the range ring you see while placing one bends outward wherever the ground falls away, so you can site a Gattling Cannon on a hill and see exactly what it covers.
- Rocket Buggies left on a hill stay on the hill. When a friend nearby got shot, every buggy around it went after the shooter, and after anything else in reach, even a tank down below that the edge of the hill hid. A buggy has to see what it fires at, so it drove to the lip to get a look, sat there unable to shoot, and then went down the slope and died. A unit that needs a clear view to fire now only answers back at what it can see from where you parked it. A buggy that picks up a salvage crate still drives to the crate.
- The five soldiers in an Overlord's battle bunker live through the Overlord's death again. A rule added in this build, that nobody gets out of a vehicle which is itself being carried, caught the bunker too, so all five died on the spot where they used to take half damage and climb out.
- Pro Rules hold for the nuke and the carpet bomb whoever fires them. The refusal stopped the recharge but not the missile: a computer player or a map script could launch a neutron missile the rules forbid, and because the timer never started, launch it again straight away.
- A Spectre gunship called in by the computer or a map script circles its target. Its aim point and its gun's aim point were only set for a gunship you called yourself, so theirs lined up on the corner of the map and walked across from there before the howitzer could fire.
- A campaign's scripted drop from a B-52 or a cargo plane already in the air uses that plane. The instruction to leave the plane alone reached the game as a lifetime of zero, and a second transport was made at the drop point.
- A radar upgrade finished while its building was knocked out counted twice, and you kept the radar after the building was gone. It counts once.
- A unit built to survive on one hit point survives armour too. The cap was applied to the raw shot, before armour multiplied it, so anything weak to the weapon still died. The Battle Bus had it worse: the killing blow ran its death, then its second life revived it anyway.
- A building cleared by a Neutron Shell forgets the stealthed GLA soldiers it held. It kept counting them, so the Rangers who moved in next could be walked in on by an enemy and thrown out.
- A Point Defence Laser leads a fast missile the way its data says it should. The predicted position was worked out and then the distance was measured to where the missile already was. It also stopped firing one last shot at a missile that had just left its range.
- An Angry Mob bought at a barracks walks out one at a time, as the stagger in its data asks. The delay between members was calculated and thrown away, so the whole mob appeared at the door at once. A mob that loses its leader can take back the stragglers, which nothing could do before because every name in the list was skipped.
- A missile fooled by flares goes for the nearest flare of the last volley. It weighed the newest flare several times over and never looked at the others.
- A toppling building crushes the strip across its fall at any angle. The line was only square to the fall when the building fell along an axis; at forty-five degrees it ran down the length of the building and missed the units beside it.
- A transport killed by the blast of a Terrorist riding in it puts the other passengers out. They were handed back to a vehicle that had already died and were deleted with it, unseen.
- Something that knocks a unit off its feet lets it stand again. The flailing pose could outlast the stun and stay for good.
- A tank thrown by a carpet bomb or a MOAB is out of your hands until it comes down. A tank hit where it stood got its controls back on the very next frame, before it had left the ground, and anything already in the air got them back at the top of its arc, so it could drive in mid-air. Now the stun lasts until it lands and a few frames after, and a tank that comes down on its roof, in water it cannot drive through, on a cliff or off the edge of the map is lost, the way the game was written to do and never did. Over one carpet bomb, 16 of 20 units that lived through it came down still stunned where none did before, and one Paladin landed on its back. Replays recorded before this change play out differently.
- Wreckage and bodies that should not bounce, don't. The data tells debris and a soldier flung by a blast to lie where they land, and fifteen entries say so, but the game never read the number and bounced all of it the same. Replays recorded before this change play out differently.
- A Sentry Drone with its gun shoots what comes near it. Two seconds after you park it the drone turns invisible, and from then on it watched enemies walk past without a shot until you gave it an order: EA's data left out the one word that lets a hidden unit open fire on its own, and the line's own comment says what the word was for. Two parked drones with three Rebels walking by and a Technical pulling up beside them used to leave all four untouched; now none of the four is left. Firing still gives the drone away, as it always did. The same goes for the Air Force, Laser and Superweapon generals' drones. Replays recorded before this change play out differently.

## The nine generals, as their own data meant them

Every Zero Hour general is a copy of a base faction with changes on top, and the copies missed things. Each general had at least three; they are all fixed, and a replay recorded before this change plays back differently.

- Air Force, Laser and Superweapon: the Avenger no longer drives off after whoever shoots it. It holds position the way every other support vehicle does.
- Air Force: the Avenger wears the Avenger's armour. It had been left on the older anti-air armour, so jet missiles did 30% to it instead of 23%.
- Air Force: a King Raptor with Countermeasures gets the armour that comes with the flares. It only ever got the flares.
- Air Force: a Saboteur in your Supply Center steals money and nothing else. The building was marked as a power plant, so the same Saboteur also blacked out your base.
- Air Force and Superweapon: nothing can shoot at the gas cloud an Aurora leaves behind. Enemy defences used to waste their fire on it.
- Air Force and Superweapon: the Microwave Tank stops showing a Composite Armor icon for an upgrade those generals cannot buy.
- Superweapon: the EMP Patriot stops knocking out its own side. Every missile leaves an EMP burst where it lands, and since EA's first patch that burst spared only your buildings, so your own and your allies' vehicles under it went dark and any of your aircraft flying beside the target fell out of the sky. Three Comanches hovering on an enemy Helix used to go down with it in the Patriot's first volley; now the Helix goes down alone. An EMP mine's burst leaves allied aircraft and an ally's buildings alone too, which it always meant to. Replays with an EMP Patriot or an EMP mine in them, recorded before this, can play out differently.
- American soldiers killed by gamma toxin scream in English. Rangers, Colonel Burton, Missile Defenders, Pathfinders and pilots of the Air Force, Laser and Superweapon generals died with a GLA rebel's voice, and Chinese infantry of the Tank, Infantry and Nuke generals did the same; Black Lotus now dies with a woman's voice.
- Laser: a Humvee plays its door sound once. Every soldier getting in or out played it twice.
- Laser: the Laser Turret's anti-air and assist shots are its own blue beam with its own sound, not a Laser Paladin's.
- Tank: the Emperor's propaganda speaker gets louder with Subliminal Messaging, like the Overlord's. It never read the upgrade at all.
- Tank: a Helix shows its Napalm Bomb once you have bought it.
- Infantry and Nuke: a Nuke Cannon fires neutron shells only after you buy Neutron Shells. Both generals' cannons came with them from the first shot.
- Infantry: the Minigunner can be run over like every other soldier.
- Infantry: a Tank Hunter's icons show Nationalism and Fanaticism, the two upgrades that change him, where one read a raw text label.
- Nuke: the Battlemaster and the Overlord drop icons for upgrades that do not exist, and the Overlord shows Isotope Stability, which it does have.
- Toxin: the Tunnel Defender fires one rocket at a time. It carries a ground rocket and an air rocket, and each kept its own reload, so with a tank and a helicopter both in range it shot at the two together.
- Toxin and Demolitions: Rebels, Terrorists and Tunnel Defenders show the upgrades they really have (Anthrax Beta and Gamma, Booby Trap) and not Camouflage, which the Toxin general cannot buy.
- Demolitions: a Stinger Site's demolition charge goes off once, at full strength, and only when you blow it up yourself. A suicide set it off twice, an ordinary death set off the suicide charge, and poison set off nothing.
- Demolitions: the Stinger Site and the Tunnel Network show the Demolitions upgrade that arms them.
- Stealth: a Rebel stays hidden when he is hit. Only firing gives him away, as on every other stealth soldier.
- Stealth: a Palace whose garrison opens fire can be seen. The soldiers inside shot from cover without ever showing it.
- Stealth: the Bomb Truck shows Anthrax Beta and the fake Arms Dealer shows Camo Netting.

A second pass went through the same nine generals and the base units they build, and found more.

- Laser and Superweapon: supply Chinooks unload at the speed the plain Chinook got in the last official patch. Both generals' Chinooks had kept the old, slower times, so their whole income ran 3 to 4% behind every other American general.
- Every Stinger soldier fires one rocket at a time. Its ground rocket and air rocket reloaded separately, so switching targets fired a second one straight away.
- Toxin: a Terrorist shot dead no longer sets off the Terrorists around him. His anthrax charge went off on any death and hurt his friends, each of them then died by explosion and fired a full dynamite pack, and a whole squad went up from one bullet.
- Toxin: the demo trap goes off after its one second warning like every other trap, and an unfinished trap shot down by the enemy stays a dud.
- Every demo trap ignores toxin and radiation puddles. An enemy Toxin Tractor spraying nearby used to set it off with nobody there.
- Demolitions: a Scud Launcher that is simply destroyed fires the small demolition blast, not the 500 damage suicide charge.
- Demolitions: a Terrorist blows up when a tank runs him over, as every other general's Terrorist does. He only went off on a suicide.
- Demolitions: tanks and infantry killed by rockets or shells die their own way, with a wreck, instead of being flung like a Terrorist and sinking into the ground with his scream.
- Demolitions: the Stinger Site's soldiers carry the Demolitions charge like the rest of that general's infantry, and a Combat Bike's Terrorist killed by anthrax Gamma still goes off.
- A heroic Quad Cannon can be jammed by an ECM Tank. Its health grew past the most jamming it could take.
- A heroic Toxin Tractor sprays the stronger poison, and now dies leaving the stronger puddle too. The Toxin Tractor left the weakest one even when it was not heroic.
- Infantry and Nuke: a deployed Nuke Cannon fires its first shell at the target you gave it. It used to swap to the nearest enemy. Its wreck leaves one radiation pool, not two stacked.
- Inferno Cannon shells always explode where they land. Shells that came down on a low arc or over a roof vanished without a hit, and never counted toward the firestorm.
- A Dragon with Black Napalm can flame a soldier standing right against it, like one without.
- Artillery Barrage shells stop blowing each other up on the way down.
- The Overlord and the Emperor keep their hull on the ground target while the Gattling turret shoots at aircraft.
- Tank: the Emperor's Gattling turret starts Veteran like everything else the Tank general builds.
- The generals' ECM Tanks jam only when you tell them to. They used to wander off to jam anything that came near.
- A China supply truck weighs as much as a truck. It weighed as much as a soldier, and every blast threw it across the map.
- A TOW Humvee stops using its anti-air reach against ground targets while it is attacking.
- A heroic pilot walks faster, not slower.
- A tank crushed by an Overlord no longer lets a pilot out, on the three generals' Microwave Tanks and the Laser Crusader.
- Rangers still show hits after Chemical Suits, and the support planes still show hits after Countermeasures.
- The Toxin Rebel no longer shows AP Bullets, which does nothing for his toxin gun.
- Sounds that were wrong or missing: the Fire Base's selection click, the Microwave Tank's engine, the Sentry Drone setting off, the construction loop on the Fire Base, Patriot and Strategy Center, the level 3 Spectre's voices and gun, the Stealth Fighter's and the MiG's low-fuel warnings, the Alpha Aurora's own voice lines, the Internet Center's selection sound, the Minigunner's anti-air gun, the Chinese carpet bomber's engine, and a voice when you order Gattling Tanks, Minigunners, Assault Troop Crawlers, Overlords or Emperors onto an aircraft.
- Icons that were wrong or missing: Supply Lines moves from the Supply Center to the Chinook, which is what it speeds up; the Ambulance shows Advanced Training, the Spectre shows Countermeasures and its portrait, the Laser Avenger shows Composite Armor, the China Command Center shows Radar, the pilot drops two upgrades he never had, and the Toxin Stinger Site, Scud Storm, Marauder and Combat Bike and the Stealth Scud Storm show their anthrax upgrade.
- Demolitions: the Rebel says his line when he plants a booby trap, the Combat Bike's button shows its icon, and a Worker on a bike dies in the Demolitions explosion.
- Toxin: a Stinger missile shot down by a laser burns up with the effect the plain one has.
- The Stealth general's Quad Cannon costs 800 like the other GLA versions. The price change had missed it.
- The Scud Storm fires when its timer says so and not before. There was a way to make it launch all nine missiles early.
- Infantry and Nuke: buying Mines at the Command Center no longer takes Frenzy off its buttons, and the Infantry general keeps his own Carpet Bomb there. The Infantry barracks keeps its own capture-building upgrade after Mines as well.
- The Nuke MiG upgrade can be started on low power, like every other upgrade that silo sells.
- The ECM Tank has an Attack Move button and the Troop Crawlers have Guard.
- The three American generals' Paradrop drops their own Rangers, and a destroyed Particle Uplink lets their own Rangers out of the rubble. Both used to give the plain American Ranger, who could not be double-click selected with the rest.
- A Saboteur thrown off a dying Combat Bike is thrown, instead of vanishing.
- The Alpha Aurora's fuel-air blast goes off where the bomb lands, not in the air above the first roof it passes, and the bomb no longer hangs in the air for a frame before landing.
- An Alpha Aurora strike no longer leaves a pale green fog over the battlefield for the rest of the match. Moving the blast to the impact also set off a cloud of fuel puffs that EA's game never shows, and nothing ever ended it: a minute after one bomb it was still rolling out a fresh puff every second, and a few strikes covered the middle of a map. The bomb now goes off with EA's fireball, dust ring and scorch and leaves nothing hanging, exactly as the Air Force General's Aurora always has.
- The Alpha Aurora attacks again. Since 2.2.0 it flew straight past whatever you sent it at: an attack order, a force attack, an attack move and a guard all came to nothing, and the A key said it had nothing that could shoot. The bomb's own hit had been set to zero when its blast moved to the impact, and a plane whose only weapon promises no damage never picks a target. The same Scorpion that sat untouched at full health is destroyed by the first bomb under every one of those five orders.
- The Alpha Aurora's bomb hits for its full damage on impact again before the blast. Moving the blast to the impact had cut the bomb's own 400-point hit, so every strike did less than EA's game: an Overlord that dies to one bomb in retail drove away on 197 of 1100, and a War Factory lost 900 where it should lose 1900. Both hits land now, the bomb first and the fuel-air blast about a second later, as in retail.

A third pass went through the generals' powers, promotions and unit abilities.

- Satellite Hack I shows every enemy Command Center, including the ones built after you researched it. It only ever showed the ones standing at that moment. CIA Intelligence and Satellite Hack II now also catch what goes up while they are running.
- Frenzy no longer scouts. Clicked into the fog, it opened a 250-wide window on the enemy base for 30 seconds.
- Tank: Battlemaster Paradrop puts the tanks on the marker. The plane opened its doors 300 out, so one tank landed well short and a pair landed on top of each other.
- China, Infantry and Nuke: the computer fires the carpet bomb it pays for. It bought the Tank general's bomber, which its own Command Center could not launch, so its fifth promotion point did nothing. A Nuke player holding a captured China Command Center no longer loses the Carpet Bomb shortcut either.
- Infantry: the computer on Normal uses the Minigunner Paradrop it spends three promotion points on. Only Hard ever called it in.
- Stealth: the Saboteur disables a China Internet Center and destroys a GLA fake building, like every other Saboteur. He used to walk up to them and do nothing. He also stops ranking up from salvage crates, which no other Saboteur does.
- Demolitions: a Combat Bike keeps its buttons after the Demolitions upgrade. The upgrade took Jarmen Kell's pilot snipe away and added a suicide button that stayed grey all game.
- Demolitions: the Rebel Ambush promotion says 4, 6 and 10 rebels, which is what it brings, not 4, 8 and 16.
- Toxin: the Rebel Ambush promotion describes the toxin rebels it calls in.
- Tooltips that named the wrong countdown: the Air Force Spectre Gunship (3:00, said 4:00), the Air Force Carpet Bomb (4:00, said China's 2:30), the Leaflet Drop (5:00, said 6:00), the Stealth GPS Scrambler (3:00, said 4:00). The Spectre's third promotion keeps the gunship up 20 seconds, and now says so.
- Air Force, Laser and Superweapon: the Pathfinder and Stealth Fighter promotions stop asking for a plain American Barracks or Airfield these generals never build.
- Voices and sounds that were recorded and never played: EVA's lines for the MOAB, the Sentry Drone Gun and Neutron Shells, the Super Hacker's own building-hack line, the Hacker's line when you arm the hack, the Artillery Barrage's own explosion (it borrowed the carpet bomb's, two at a time), and Jarmen Kell's charge-planting sound, where he said his attack line twice.
- The Superweapon general's Particle Cannon glows and dies in the pink of its beam, not in the plain cannon's blue. The laser beams it throws when destroyed go up pink as well. EA never made a pink model for them, so the same beams are drawn with a pink strip in place of the blue one, and the other generals' uplinks still die in blue.
- The Particle Uplink's dish holds still while the building goes up instead of cycling its deploy animation.
- A Sneak Attack tunnel coming out of the ground shows its own portrait, and the Demolitions Bomb Truck stops showing the Demolitions upgrade icon.

## One crate, one collector

- Salvage gets collected. A wreck leaves money and a free upgrade lying on the ground, and the game asked you to spot it in the middle of the fight that made it, work out which of your units was allowed to take it, and drive that one over it by hand. Nobody does that, so most salvage on most maps timed out where it fell. The computer's units go and get it now: a unit that can still be upgraded off it goes first, however far back it is standing, and failing that the nearest one takes the cash. Dozers and harvesters are left to the job they are already earning at.
- Your units stay where you put them. The first version of salvage collection sent yours too, and a squad you had parked walked off to a wreck you never told it about. Only the computer's units go for salvage by themselves now; yours take a crate when you drive them over it.
- And the collector comes back. A line dug in across a road is idle, every man of it, so the first version of this walked the line out to the wrecks in front and left it standing there - the position lost to the crates it had just earned. A unit sent for a crate now walks back to the spot it left. Nobody with an enemy in sight goes at all, and nothing is called further than a few seconds away. Anything that dies when it fires stays where it is: a Terrorist is worth more than every crate on the map.
- The computer collects salvage too. That pass only ever looked at idle units, and a computer opponent never has one: it keeps every team on an order from the moment the team exists. So a crate could lie there for half a minute with three of its own rebels beside it and then time out, and every wreck on the field was money that only you could pick up. A unit that is busy is told about the crate now rather than ordered to it, and it takes the detour between one thing and the next before going back to what it was doing - the same way a unit walks over the wreck of something it just killed.
- Salvage waits. A crate used to disappear thirty seconds after it fell, which on most maps was before anyone could get to it: four crates from one fight, all four still lying untouched when the clock took them. They stay on the ground now until something drives over one.
- And anybody can take the money. A salvage crate could only be picked up by a unit that salvages, so one dropped among troops who could not use it was money that belonged to nobody. The parts still only go to a salvager - the weapon, the armour, the promotion - but the cash goes to whoever drives over it.
- A crate pays once per frame, not once per soldier who touched it.
- Crates pay again. In v2.2.0 no crate could be picked up at all: the Supply Drop Zone's parachute drop landed and sat there as boxes, and salvage and money crates stayed on the ground under the wheels that drove over them. The drop zone brings in its $1,500 every two minutes again, and a Humvee driven over a crate takes it.
- Salvage pays again. That pickup fix covered every crate marked as one, and the pile a wreck leaves was not among them, so a GLA tank's scrap sat on the ground under the wheels that drove over it. Driving over it takes it now: the parts go to a salvager, the cash to whoever gets there. A replay from before this, of a match where somebody drove over that pile, will not play back the same way.
- A unit that kills something carrying a crate now goes for the crate. The game noted the crate, then forgot which one before it looked, so the "pick up what I just dropped" order at seven places in the code never once fired.
- A thrown vehicle's crash damage hits the pile once, not once per unit.
- A dying unit is no longer promoted by its last kill.
- Healing no longer counts as damage, so a medic stops revealing its stealth unit.

## Circles are round, and a tunnel is one tunnel

- Circular range checks are circles now, not the square drawn around them.
- A tunnel network heals at one rate, however many entrances it has.
- GLA units take the tunnels on their own. Give a move order across the map, and if the way through your network is any shorter than the walk, the whole selection goes down the tunnel nearest it and comes up at the one nearest where you sent it, then walks the rest. An attack move does the same and comes up still attack-moving, and fresh units heading for a rally point across the map take the tunnels too, fighting on the way if they would have walked there fighting. The tunnels used to have to be almost a third shorter before anybody took them, so fresh units with a tunnel beside their barracks and one beside the rally point mostly walked anyway. Passing through does not count as resting: only units you put inside yourself are healed. The group decides together: fifteen Rebels with six places already taken all go through, where a first version sent ten underground and walked the other five. The green order line shows it: one line to the tunnel they go down and one from the tunnel they come up at to where you sent them. Twelve Rebels crossing a small map with a tunnel at each end got there in 21 seconds instead of 50, four Scorpions in 9 instead of 21. A tunnel that has just been hit is skipped, so nobody surfaces into the fire. So is one still going up or being sold: a tunnel you had only just placed used to count straight away, and units sent across the map queued up at a building site. A tunnel rising again out of a GLA hole counts as going up until it is finished, and units underground when the tunnel they went down is destroyed no longer come up at one. The computer does it too: a GLA team losing a fight near one of its tunnels falls back underground and comes up at home, which happened 10 to 25 times a match in four Hard GLA mirrors on Twilight Flame, and an attack wave takes the tunnels when they come up nearer your base than its road does. And the computer now digs them on purpose: a tunnel at home, one at the expansion it holds, and one three quarters of the way down the road its next wave will take, as long as nothing it has seen there can shoot. It does not wait for a fat bank to do it: a tunnel costs 800 and pays back on the first wave. In four Hard GLA mirrors on Twilight Flame, 10 of 49 attack waves went underground, where none had before, and beaten teams fell back through a tunnel 250 times. Units you put in a tunnel yourself stay put, and a network they have filled is left alone: the order walks. With one place free, everybody still goes through it one after the other, since a unit passing through leaves the moment it arrives. Replays recorded before this change play out differently.
- Units a move order takes through the tunnels come out still selected. Going underground took each one off your selection and nothing put it back, so a group sent across the map surfaced as nobody's and had to be picked up again by hand. A trip you order by clicking a tunnel still leaves the selection the way it always has, and picking something else while they are underground means they stay out of it.
- Builders take the tunnels to work. A GLA Worker sent to build, repair or finish a structure goes through the network when that is shorter than the walk, and carries on with the job at the far end. The same holds for the builder of any side that has tunnels. Replays recorded before this change play out differently.
- A move order onto ground your units cannot walk to goes through the tunnels. Up a cliff, across water with no bridge, the plateaus of the GLA campaign: with a tunnel on your side and one on theirs, the selection goes down and comes up there, where it used to walk to the foot of the cliff and stop, and you had to put them in the tunnel and empty it on the far side by hand. The tunnels picked are the nearest ones your units can actually walk to and from, so nobody comes up on an island next to where you sent them and stands there, and a tunnel across a river no longer counts as the way in for units on this bank. Four Rebels sent into a walled-off hollow on Dark Night, 700 short of it and 900 from the tunnel behind them, came up in it 49 seconds later. Replays recorded before this change play out differently.

## The power bar tells the truth

- An EMP on an upgraded plant no longer takes the upgrade off your grid twice.
- An EMP on a building site no longer moves power that does not exist yet.
- Loading a saved game keeps disabled power plants disabled.
- Control Rods finishing during a blackout is no longer credited twice.

## Your whole base, on one strip

- One row above the command bar carries everything your base is building anywhere.
- Ordered by time left, so it reads as the order things arrive.
- Select a building and what it is making moves to the front of that row, ahead of everything else, still soonest first among itself. It used to get a whole second row of its own under the base's, which said the same thing twice and pushed the strip further up the screen; now the head of the strip is the building you are looking at and the tail is the rest of your base.
- Click an ally's War Factory, Barracks or Airfield and its production queue comes up at the front of your own strip, counting down as it builds. It is there to look at: you cannot cancel anything in it.
- Click a picture to jump the camera there; Ctrl-click cancels it.
- One picture is one order, cut to match the command bar's artwork.
- The strip is half the size it was: it says the same thing and takes back the screen it was eating.
- Whatever is actually being built counts down inside its own picture. The ones queued behind it stay blank, because their wait depends on everything in front of them.
- A unit still rolling out of its factory takes your orders. Click a tank the moment it appears and send it anywhere, or queue a route with shift: it drives out of the door first and then goes, instead of ignoring the click until it was clear. The order replaces the rally point. A dozer told to build the instant it leaves the Command Center used to drive straight from the door to the site, through the Command Center's walls; it comes out and goes round now.
- An upgrade stays available if any selected building can still buy it.
- One right click cancels one thing, counted the moment you press.
- A build button takes a batch: Shift queues five, Ctrl twenty, both together as many as the queue will hold and the bank will pay for. Right-click with the same keys and the same number comes back out. Ten tanks used to be ten clicks. How many are still coming is written in the corner of the button, half again bigger than the price and the countdown beside it and on a plate you can actually read it against: at the size the other markings wear, the one number you check in the middle of a fight was a smudge you had to look for.
- Right-click a picture on the strip and that one order is cancelled and paid back, without walking the camera over to the building first. A build button greyed out because the bank is empty or the queue is full still takes the right click, so what is already queued on it can come back out.
- Buying an upgrade for a group starts it in all of them.
- The progress clock over a picture is steady at any frame rate.
- A group's upgrade shows its progress, whichever building is paying for it.
- Click a unit with several factories selected and it takes the shortest queue.
- A plane stays buildable while any selected airfield still has a free spot. One full airfield used to grey the button out for the whole group, even with three empty runways in the same selection.
- A general's upgrade spreads across the selected buildings instead of hitting one.
- A clicked upgrade darkens immediately and uncovers as it progresses.
- The production strip dims fresh orders the same way.
- A second worker can take over a half-built building.
- The nearest idle worker takes a new job, without yanking a busy one off.
- Hand one worker a string of jobs and it does them in the order you gave them. Hold shift and put down three buildings while it is busy; each waits on the ground as a plan, and the worker goes from the first to the second to the third. It used to take whichever was nearest when it finished, so the barracks you placed first could be the last one up. Each waiting plan now carries its turn as a number over the outline, 1 for the one going up next, counted separately for every worker that has more than one, so two dozers laying out one base never read as a single list. Only you see them.
- A worker that is building something still offers you the whole build list.
- The GLA's decoy build list toggles both ways now.
- Workers go back to collecting supplies when they finish.
- Workers and supply trucks walk to a pile up to 1400 feet away, the reach the computer opponent always had. Yours gave up at 700: when the pile beside your base ran dry they went home and stood there, with the next pile in plain sight.
- Every countdown reads real seconds and answers the game speed. That means the speed the match is really running at, measured over the last second, and not the one it was set to: a barracks that says 10s in a match holding 30 frames a second says 30s once a crowded fight drags it down to 10, because that is how long you will wait for it. Build buttons, the production queue, buildings going up, the bars over your units and the bomb timers all read it.
- The clock and frame-rate readout is drawn on top of everything.
- The superweapon countdowns are pictures now, not a list of names: the same strip treatment, in the top right corner, under that readout. Each one wears its own seconds and a sweeping clock, the next to land sits at the right hand end, and a seventh starts a second row under the first.
- The superweapon countdowns are back in the top right corner while you play. They left when the row of players across the top arrived, and stayed gone when that row did, so a player had to hold Tab to see when the enemy's nuke would land. Watching a match or a replay, they stay on the Tab scoreboard and off the battlefield.
- Those countdowns stand in the command bar's steel. They were still sitting in the old bar's tray art, each in a flat box of its owner's colour. Now they share one steel tray with the production queue's thin rim, and the frame round each picture is the colour of the player whose weapon it is, one solid band with the tray's steel between two of them. A charged one breathes every 2.4 seconds, slowing at both ends: its frame swells toward white while a wash of its owner's colour rises over the picture, and both sink back. Every superweapon has a cell of its own, six to a row, where anything past eighteen used to fold into a count. The tray is cut to each row: a last row of two stands in steel two cells wide, against the edge of the screen. The tray grows with HUD Size, and at every step the steel between two pictures is one width along a row and down the rows. With the corner box switched off it sits in the corner itself.
- The small figures on a button or a picture stand on a solid plate inside the frame: the key, the price, the seconds, the power and the count on every command button, the countdown and the "x5" on a queue picture, the seconds on a superweapon. They used to sit flush in the corner on a see-through plate, so the steel frame was drawn over the first digit's left edge and a pale picture showed through the number. A figure too long for its button, a four-digit price at the smallest HUD Size, is set a size smaller until it fits. A countdown is never the one that shrinks: a "103s" too wide for its picture is written "103" at the size of the "79s" beside it.
- Every button's corner markings - the hotkey, the price, the time, the number queued - sit flush in the corners instead of floating a few pixels inside them, and are set a size smaller than the button's own text: four labels at full size were eating the picture they were meant to annotate.
- The messages that ran down the top left corner, out of money or a structure under attack, are lines of a feed standing on the radar now, the way Dota's kill feed stands on its minimap. Each line sits on a black band that thins out over the battlefield. A line about a player opens on his general's flag with his name in his colour; the rest open on a gold mark. A smoke signal or a beacon goes in, and so does a player beaten or gone, for a player and a watcher alike. Six lines at most, ten seconds each, the newest at the bottom, and the feed steps up over the production queue's row whenever that is out. Missed something? Press Enter: while the chat is open the last twelve lines come back, however old they are, and the chat moves up to stand clear of them. A line with no player opens on a steel plate the size of a flag with a gold exclamation mark lying across it, so every line's words start in the same column.
- A superweapon's wait is written in plain seconds - 200, not 3:20 - so it compares with every other countdown on the screen at a glance. Once it is charged the number goes away entirely and the picture itself breathes in your colour, which is the one thing you want to catch out of the corner of your eye.
- The countdown inside a strip picture moved down into its bottom left corner and carries the unit with it - 45s, 200s - on a translucent plate. In the middle of the picture it sat on the one thing the picture is there to show, and the plate it lost when it was in the middle earns its place down in the corner, where a pale cameo was swallowing the digits and their shadow together. This applies to the superweapon countdowns in the top right as well.
- Every picture in the row stands in a tray - the same tray your general's powers stand in down in the corner, your own side's copy of it, so the American, Chinese and GLA strips each carry their own metal. The tray is turned back to front, because that bar grows out of the right hand corner and the strip grows out of the left, so its heavy rail leads the row instead of standing between every pair of pictures. It is never stretched to fit, and side by side the trays close up behind each other by exactly what that bar closes its own up by, so a row of them reads as one run of metal rather than a line of loose boxes. Stacked they stand clear of each other instead: a picture in a column carries its whole tray, with none of the one above it cutting across its top edge. The strip is flush against the left edge, and a black rectangle laid over the battlefield is gone.
- The queue stands up out of the corner instead of lying across the bottom of the screen. It is a column now, five pictures high, growing upward from just above the command bar with the next thing to arrive at the bottom of it - so the one picture you actually read is always in the same place, a thumb's width above the buttons, and it does not move when six more tanks are ordered. What is left over closes the column as a sixth cell wearing a "+N". A busy base used to lay its whole queue across the battlefield; now it costs one picture's width of screen, whatever it has coming. Playing under the new steel command bar it lies down again, as a row: a steel tray on the radar's side standing just over the under-attack light, the twin of the general's powers on the other side, the next thing to arrive against the screen's left edge and the rest running right. Each picture sits in a framed cell the size of a power's, so the two trays read as a pair.
- A slot in it is a slot of that bar to the pixel, at every resolution - the size is measured off the bar itself rather than guessed - so the queue is read at a glance instead of squinted at: these were postage stamps a third of that size. Ten tanks ordered back to back are one picture with an x10 in its corner rather than ten copies of the same tank eating the row, so what is left of the row still says what else is coming.
- The buildings your workers are raising stand in that same column, not in one of their own: a war factory going up is a picture in the queue at the point its time puts it, between the tank that lands before it and the one that lands after. One column is the whole answer to what your base has coming, read bottom to top in the order it arrives, and it no longer takes a second strip of screen to say the slower half of it. Each site carries its own sweep and the seconds it still needs. Click one and the camera goes there - a half-finished building under fire on the far side of the map is one click away instead of a hunt across the minimap - and none of them can be cancelled by mistake: a building already standing is sold or lost, never cancelled, so Ctrl over it does nothing.
- The superweapon strip in the top right stands in the same metal, the right way round this time: it is anchored to the right hand edge and grows leftwards, which is the way that bar in the corner grows, so its rail closes the row against the edge of the screen. The black rectangle behind those pictures is gone with it, and the countdowns are the size of the ones down in the corner rather than half of it.
- Nothing in a row of trays lies over the picture in the tray beside it any more. A slot used to slide an eighth of a tray back under its neighbour so a row would read as one run of metal, and what it actually did was drop that neighbour's rail across the left edge of every cameo but the last - visible on the general's powers along the bottom right and on the superweapon countdowns along the top. They step a whole tray now, sideways as well as upward, which is what the queue column down the left has always done.
- A game opens with nothing selected, so your first click is not a rally point.
- Your general's powers no longer climb the right hand edge of the screen. They sit three abreast, filling from the bottom corner where the first one has always been and wrapping upward, so a general holding eleven of them still reads as a block instead of a ladder from the radar to the sky. Each row stands in steel only as wide as the powers in it, the way the superweapon countdowns do: seven powers are two rows of three with a single one on top against the screen's edge, where the tray used to be a full three by three with an empty dark corner. The keys follow the same shape: the first press picks a row, the second picks the power in it, so every power is two keys away - F1 F1 for the one in the corner, F2 F1 for the row above it. Only the keys you can press next are written on the pictures, so you are never reading eleven labels to find one, and Escape drops a row you thought better of - so does clicking a power with the mouse, and so does four seconds' pause, so a key hit by mistake in a fight never turns your next one into a superweapon. Rows with nothing in them yet cannot be picked at all: early on, when you hold three powers, only the row you have answers a key.
- The second key of that pair works now. Anything that touched the interface at all - a promotion, a unit rolling out, a building captured somewhere across the map - was quietly forgetting the row you had just picked, and in a real fight that happens several times a second, so the pair almost never completed and the powers looked as though they answered no key at all.
- The key written on a power is drawn over its cooldown sweep instead of under it. A power still charging had its label buried by the black wedge sweeping across the picture, which is exactly when you want to know which key it is.
- The tilde key opens the general's promotions, and Escape closes them - Escape used to bring up the pause menu on top of the open screen, leaving the small exit button in its corner as the only way out. The screen is five columns wide and 1 to 5 now name them: each number sits in the corner of the science that column would sell you next, and pressing it twice buys that one - once to mark, once to spend, so a point is never gone to a key hit in a hurry. Buy the top of a chain and the number drops onto the next rung down, so a whole column goes in with one finger while the fight carries on behind you.
- `S` stops your units again; the key was simply never bound.
- Every button carries its build, research or cooldown time in the corner.
- A single selected unit wears a gold bar filling toward its next rank.
- Timings everywhere: buildings, queues, superweapon charge, upgrades being researched.
- A charge bar now says how many seconds are left.
- Aircraft always show how many attack runs they have left.
- The corner readout separates game time from real time, and sim rate from fps. It also says DX11 or DX9 after the frame rate, which is what your machine is actually drawing with: a graphics card that cannot start Direct3D 11 falls back to 9 without a word, and a frame rate means nothing until you know which of the two it belongs to. The 64-bit game writes x64 after it, so a screenshot says which build took it.
- Your computer's own clock leads that readout, so you can see the time without leaving the match.
- Pausing stops both clocks.
- Watching a match, hold Tab and every player's queue is on his row of the scoreboard, on the line under the name, ahead of his superweapons: the three that land soonest with their seconds and a count of the rest. Buildings going up on the ground are in there too, so a player answering an attack with three war factories is visible while the concrete is still wet. It used to be eight rows of pictures piled up the left edge of the screen for the whole match, over the battlefield. Knocked out and staying to watch, Tab gives you this same board, everybody in full; it used to keep your old side's view, with the enemy as names and nothing else.
- Every row and every countdown stands in real metal, cut from the same tray the general's powers sit in down in the corner - the side your command bar is showing. Watching a match there is no bar of your own, so the superweapon countdowns used to fall back to a flat black box for the whole match; now they wear the metal of the player you are watching, and follow it when you switch seats.
- Watching, the command bar is down to the radar. The list of players it held is the board on the right and the Tab scoreboard now. Pick a player to follow in the camera panel and you are looking at him, his money and his side's steel, the way his button in the list did; pick nobody for everybody. Picking drops whatever you had selected, so the promotions on screen are that player's and not the owner of the tank you last clicked. The portrait panel in the right corner comes up when you select something and goes when you click empty ground, so an empty match leaves the bottom of the screen to the battle.
- Watching, the bars over the buildings are everyone's too - what each factory is turning out, and how long the superweapons have left. An observer used to see none of it.
- Watching, the camera can drive itself. Two drop-downs in the camera panel on the right, above the board, say who has it and which player you follow, and you can follow nobody. The Director goes to the fight with the most on the line over the last five seconds, where a dead Overlord counts for more than six riflemen being shot at and anything happening to a superweapon counts three times over. It stays with that fight eight seconds, leaves after four for one two and a half times the size, and after twenty seconds on the same fight, or as soon as the fight has burnt down to half of what it was, a third bigger is enough. So a 4v4 is watched like a broadcast rather than hunted for, and it does not hop between spots every few seconds. Any general's power used on the map outranks every fight. For a nuke the camera goes straight to the target and waits there for it to come down and stays through the blast. The particle cannon's beam is followed along its whole sweep, and a Scud Storm or a carpet bomb is watched until the last explosion has died down. Then it goes back to the fighting. The camera glides between places and slows into each one, and only cuts when the next one is across most of the map. When nobody is shooting it no longer parks on empty ground: it goes round what is worth seeing, an army on the march, a superweapon, a base going up, the rest of a base, the dearest first and nine seconds each, and does not come back to one of the last three while there is anything else to show. With a player picked it keeps to his fights and his own armies and buildings. Player camera shows his screen: a human player's camera comes over the network five times a second, smoothed in between, and a computer player, who has no camera, gets the Director narrowed to his own fights. Scroll with the keys or a drag, turn the view or click the radar and the camera is yours again, still following the same player. The edge of the screen does not scroll while it drives: the panel on the right stands on that edge, and reaching for it used to hand the camera back to you, so the Director stopped following the fighting the first time you went to change a setting. In a replay with the recorded camera switched on in the options, the player you follow still drives the camera the way his button in the old list did.
- Watching, the Director climbs over a wide battle until the whole of it is on the screen, and comes back down to your own height when it moves on or you take the camera. The mouse wheel still zooms while it drives, and it keeps what you chose. It stops short of the map's border too, where a fight on the edge used to come with a black corner of nothing beside it. A nuke or a Scud Storm is shown leaving its silo even when the Director has only just arrived at another fight; before, the launch was over by the time it was allowed to move. Rewinding a replay leaves your camera settings as they were, where a rewind used to hand you back a free camera with nobody followed. And following one player while a superweapon wiped out everything he had at the spot could close the game. It no longer does.
- The Director stands further back. It used to frame the unit the shots came from and miss the tanks fighting just beside it; now it watches every place from 200 paces over your own height, so the whole fight and what is around it are on the screen. Its climbs and descents take a couple of seconds and ease in and out, and gliding over hills no longer pumps the picture in and out over every ridge. A superweapon or a general's power is one shot now: when the screen split to show the silo and the target, the camera used to drop low when the split closed and then spend ten seconds climbing back up, and it comes out of the split at the height it was heading for.
- Watching can be filmed. Start the game with `-directorrecord` and the Director has the camera from the first second, with nothing over the picture but the radar, and the whole match goes into one video; the game closes itself when the match is decided or the replay runs out. Beside `-replay` it turns an old match into footage, and beside four computer players it films a match nobody played. It plays the match twice. The first time there is no picture and the game runs as fast as the machine goes, 8,000 frames of a four-player match in 17 seconds, writing down where each fight starts and when each superweapon fires; the second time it films, and the camera is already at a fight four seconds before the first shot instead of arriving halfway through it. When two fights are about to start far apart the screen splits three seconds before the second begins, and only when the first run saw the Director's own fight go on for the whole time the split will be up, so the second picture never sweeps in and straight back out. A skirmish the first run saw come to nothing is not cut to, and neither is a special power that hurts nobody: an Air Force general's spy satellite every minute used to pull the camera to empty ground, where a carpet bomb or an artillery strike still gets it from the launch. Each half keeps its own fight. The main camera used to leave for the second half's fight as it began, and the two halves glided past each other over empty ground and swapped places; now it takes that fight over only once its own is finished, and the second half closes as it does. The two runs compare the whole state of the game every 30 seconds, and if they ever disagree the rest is filmed the old way, finding fights as they happen. A skirmish without `-seed` is handed one, the same for both runs. The film opens on every player at once: the screen cut into one picture per player along lines that meet round the radar, two players on a diagonal and four on an X, each picture on that player's base. Every picture gets the same share of the screen, whatever its shape: the lines used to stand at a fixed 45 degrees, and on a 16:9 screen three players got a sliver of 14% at the top and two big halves below, and at 21:9 the sliver was 10%. Now each has a third. Seven seconds in they sweep away to the Director's single view, which stays on the first player's base until something happens instead of gliding across empty ground to another one. When two real fights far apart are both worth watching, the radar slides out of its corner, then the second fight sweeps in from the corner along the screen's diagonal, carrying the radar in a frame of the same line to the middle, one fight on each side with its own camera gliding to it. Going back is the same film run backwards: the second fight sweeps out with the radar, and the radar slides back into its corner. The lines are the Reforged gold on a dark edge, so they read on snow and at night, and they run straight into the radar's frame. In its corner the radar sits flush against the bottom and left of the picture with a thin gold edge on its top and right only, where it used to stand in a heavy frame a few pixels in from the corner. Under each line and round the radar lies a soft band of the Reforged blue. The pictures slide into place first, and only then does the gold draw out from where the lines meet, over most of a second, with the band fading in behind it and the radar's frame tracing its gold round the map once the radar has arrived; going out, the gold pulls back in before the pictures leave. The opening plays it the same way from the first player's full view. While the screen stays cut a bright white-gold light runs out along every line every five seconds, the opening included. At first all of this ran inside the half second the pictures take to slide, and on film the lines were already whole by the third frame. While the screen is cut, each picture frames its own fight: the camera puts it in the biggest round space its slice has clear of the lines and the radar, and rises until the whole fight fits there with room to spare, never lower than the Director flies and never more than three times as high. A fight that spreads is let out smoothly; one that shrinks does not make the picture pump in and out. The single view rises and settles over a second and a half when it reaches a fight, where it used to glide in for seconds and then step back up in a third of one, and the hand-over between the cut screen and the single view is one camera: the opening's last picture used to jump sideways about fifty paces the moment it became the Director's. When the Director moves onto the second half's fight, that half is the one that stays: it widens to fill the screen and carries straight on as the single view, where the other half used to fill the screen and the camera then cut across the map. The opening frames each player's base the same way. A split holds at least six seconds after its second picture is all the way in and ends once the second fight has burnt down to a quarter of the first, and the picture then stays whole four seconds even for a fight the first run saw coming, so it does not flicker between one and two. Inside a split no picture ever jumps. A split ends the usual way after eighteen seconds, or the moment either half's fight is over or moves on to somewhere else, and only then does the camera go where the next fight is; in an eight-player film one split stayed up for three minutes while the main half was thrown across the map to six new fights, once by 2,900 paces as it was still sweeping in. The two fights have to be further apart than either half's picture is wide once it has risen to fit its fight, so no piece of ground shows in both: at a fixed 660 the two halves of a 1v1 showed the same bridge from neighbouring angles. A crowd beside the Director's fight that the first run saw as part of it does not split the screen either; that was the Director's own fight spreading, and the second half swept in for two seconds before the camera followed it there. Only the video shows the other pictures; the window keeps the Director's own fight under the lines. Two bases going up are not a fight, and the split ends the moment the camera's own fight and the second one turn out to be the same. The pictures go straight into ffmpeg as they are drawn, so an hour of 1080p does not need tens of gigabytes of disk first; put ffmpeg beside the game or on your PATH, because without it nothing is recorded and the game says so and closes. While the screen is split the score bar slides up off the top with the radar's slide, so the pictures have the whole screen, and it comes back down when they go; it hangs in the radar's own frame, a thin gold edge on a soft blue halo down its sides and along its foot. The film carries a score bar across the top in the Reforged colours, the match clock in a tab over it and every side in one row, "Team A vs Team B vs" a player alone: a card a player, his colour along its top, his name in that colour, his general (a computer player goes by his general with his difficulty under it, where every one of them used to read "Hard AI"), then on one line his cash and what his army cost, the army's figure labelled so the two never get mixed up; the bar is 77 rows of a 720p picture for a 1v1 where its first cut took 126: the general or difficulty stands at the right end of the name's line instead of on a line of its own, and the clock, the armies' bar and the colour strip along each card are thinner. A player's name past about fourteen letters ends in an ellipsis, so one player called thirty letters long no longer widens every card on the bar or runs into the general beside his own. The plates over the opening's and a split's pictures and the Defeated and Victory banners cut a long name the same way, each to the room it has; a computer player's general is always written out whole. A team's cards sit together under its name and its army's total. Under the whole row is one bar the armies pull on, each player's share in his colour and a gold mark at the middle when two sides play, so whoever is past it is ahead. From five players on the cards stand in two rows, a team never broken across them, and each card keeps to one line, the name and the army's value, so the type stays at its full size: eight players in one row had shrunk it to letters six pixels tall that nobody could read in motion. Eight alone take 94 rows of a 720p picture, a 4v4 with its two team names 130. A dark colour like purple is lightened where it is text, so it reads on the bar's ground, and the gold player's colour gets a dark edge two pixels wide even at 720p so it does not melt into the gold lines. A skirmish can be staged in any shape now, 4v1 or three players alone beside a pair: `-team 4 0` puts slot 4 on team 0. The opening seats teammates next to each other and names their team on their plates, and a split names a team as "Team A" where a player alone keeps his own name. Each picture of the opening names its player on a plate hanging just over his command centre, and the plate stays on the building as the camera rises and the pictures sweep away. The name sits over his general and team in smaller type, and the plate stays wholly inside its picture: in an eight-player opening the lines used to cut it down to "ank General Team A", and in a 4v1 the top one hid under the score bar. Each half of a split names the players in its fight, in a plate inside its own slice clear of the lines and the radar, and while the halves sweep in it rides above its fight, where it used to slide down over it. It is sized from the screen's height and reads the same at 720p and 1080p, and the pictures under it are framed below it, so no base or fight sits behind the bar. Any recorded clip also plays its animations at their real speed now. The animation clock used to move by however long each picture took to save, and a slow disk made every explosion in the footage run fast. In a two-player film each half of the split belongs to one player for the whole match, the first player in the upper left and his opponent in the lower right from the opening's two bases on, each half on wherever that player's own guns are firing or, when they are quiet, on his base and his army; the screen used to show whichever two fights were hottest, often both in one player's half, and a 1v1 now splits about every twenty seconds where an earlier rule had cut it to a few times a match. The radar draws a view box for each half while the screen is cut, where it used to mark only the upper left one.
- The film shows who fired what. When a player uses a general's power, a small flag drops out from under his card in the score bar carrying the power's icon, hangs there four seconds and pulls back up; a superweapon's flag is edged in gold. Every power a player fires gets its flag the moment he fires it, the command centre's spy satellite included, so a power with nothing to film still shows on the bar, and a player who fires two in quick succession has them wait their turn under his card. An upgrade he finishes buying drops its own flag the same way, behind any power waiting there. A power that lands takes the camera straight away: the Director used to finish the four seconds it gives any shot first, and in the first cup film a spy satellite was on screen three seconds late. With the first run's notes the camera is already waiting where the power will land before it is fired. A superweapon is filmed where it hits, not in its silo. Two powers landing far apart at once split the screen, one half each, however recently the last split went: fights still split only rarely. A power holds the camera thirty seconds at most; a particle cannon fired into the middle of the map kept it there for eight minutes in the first cup film while every other power went by unfilmed, because the battle round its target never stopped. Riflemen shooting at riflemen count for a quarter of what they did, so a small infantry skirmish rarely takes the camera from tanks, buildings or a power. The single view no longer carries an "X vs Y" plate under the score bar; the halves of a split and the opening keep theirs. A player knocked out no longer blinks out of the bar. His card flashes red with a line drawn through his name, and a second later it closes while the cards beside it slide into the room it leaves, where the whole row used to jump on the frame he lost. At the same moment a banner opens across the lower part of the picture with his name and general under a red Defeated. The match no longer cuts off on the frame the last card falls, either: the film runs three and a half seconds past the decision, so the last defeat plays out and the winner's banner comes up in gold beneath it.
- Watching, tick Fog of war under the camera and you see what the player you follow sees and nothing more: black where he has never been, grey where he has been, none of the enemy he cannot see, and an enemy base as he last saw it. Stealth goes by his eyes too: a Jarmen Kell he has not found is gone from the screen and the radar, and his own stealthed units show the way they show him. It stays on whoever drives the camera, until you switch it off or follow nobody. Switch to another player and his fog comes in whole, shadows included: a building he has never seen casts nothing on his grey ground. An observer used to have no fog at all: the whole map, all match, with no way to see the game the way one of its players was seeing it.
- An ally, and anyone watching, now sees a stealthed unit's muzzle flash, its promotion and the cash it earns - if they can see the unit at all. Those three asked 'is it mine' instead of 'can I see it', so allies and observers got nothing.
- Watching, the match opens on the middle of the map. An observer has no start position, so the game looked for the camera waypoint of "player zero", did not find it, and fell back to the corner of the map: every match watched from the stands began pointed at empty ground in the south west.
- Watching, you can click a stealth building. The rule that stops you selecting an enemy you cannot see never asked who was asking, so an observer, and a player already knocked out, could see a stealthed structure on the map and not pick it up to read it. A spectator commands nothing and sees everything already, so there is nothing there to give away.
- Watching, the promotions every general has bought are on screen the whole match: a strip of them in the bottom right corner, one row per player in his own colour. There was nowhere else to find this out - the promotion screen filled itself in with the watcher's own player, who has no general and no command sets at all, so a spectator got a blank screen.
- Watching, clicking one player's unit watches him, and clicking empty ground puts the whole match back. The screen wears his side's metal and the promotions on the right are his alone. The promotion key then opens his promotion screen, on his side's artwork, with his skill points and his rank - greyed out, because they are not yours to spend. It is the same size a player's is; picking his unit rebuilt the bar under a watcher and the screen came up a quarter narrower, its pictures squeezed thin. With nothing selected the key does nothing; there is no one general to show.
- A general who has taken Artillery Barrage to level 3 shows one Artillery Barrage on the promotions strip, not three copies of the same picture in a row.
- The strips close up again: across a row the trays slide an eighth of a tray under each other, so a row reads as one run of metal. The cameos never overlap each other, because every tray goes down before any picture does.
- Watching, the top left corner is clear. The promotions and superweapon shelves that stood there are gone: every general's promotions and superweapons are on the Tab scoreboard.
- Watching, an observer's board sits on the right of the screen, built from the command bar's own steel, and the steel is the watched side's: grey for America, red for China, sand for GLA, changing when you select a unit of another side: every player's general, his name in his colour, the number in gold, and under it a bar in the same colour as his name. In a two-sided match the bars used to go green for one side and red for the other, so a blue name could stand over a red bar. It opens on net worth, the cash in hand plus what everything the player has standing would cost to build again, counted twice a second, richest first. Its drop-downs close when you click anywhere else, on the battlefield, the radar or another drop-down, instead of staying open until you picked something in them.
- The number the board ranks by is yours to pick from the drop-down at its top: net worth, cash, income per minute, army value, kills and losses, the general's rank and power. The list re-sorts the moment you choose. The command bar's keys pick them too while you watch, Q W E R T Y and A, and the drop-down writes each stat's key beside it.
- A button under the list opens the armies: every player's six most expensive kinds of unit in cameo trays, with how many of each he has on the field. The generals' emblems on the board stand bare, with no tray round them.
- The drop-downs on the right open over the board instead of pushing it down the screen.
- The superweapon countdowns and the promotions each general has bought moved into a panel on the left, one row per player with his general at the head of it, where they used to be two strips down the right-hand edge.
- A superweapon coming ready or being fired is a line of that feed: whose it is, its picture and its name, then "ready to fire" in green or "launched" in red. Only a watcher used to be told, and only when one came ready.
- The feed also says when a general's power is used, when a promotion is bought, and when a superweapon, a Strategy Center, a Propaganda Center or a Palace goes up and when it is finished, each with its picture. A player is told that about himself and his allies, a watcher about everybody; a finished superweapon is told to everybody, as the announcer already did. A Spectre Gunship called from three command centers on the same frame is one line, not three. A player whose last dozer dies, or last worker for the GLA, gets a line too, since he can build nothing more until he makes another.
- Watching, the selected unit's health shows beside its portrait again, and the right-hand column (the camera and follow drop-downs, the board, the armies) and the replay strip are drawn at the command bar's scale, about 30% smaller than they were, so one HUD Size setting sizes all of it. Clicking or dragging the radar while the camera is on Director or Player used to be undone a moment later by the automatic camera; the click takes the camera now and it stays where you put it.
- Chat sits in the bottom left corner over the event feed, with room left for the whole feed so neither covers the other, the way Dota writes it: each line opens on the speaker's general's flag and his name in his colour. A conversation stays up for ten seconds after its last line and then fades out as one.
